3Parkinson’s Disease Detection Using Voice and Speech—Systematic Literature Review
Ronak Khatwad1, Suyash Tiwari1, Yash Tripathi1, Ajay Nehra1 and Ashish Sharma2*
1Dept. of CSE, IIIT Kota, MNIT Jaipur, Jaipur, India
2Dept. of CSE, Manipal University Jaipur, Jaipur, India

Abstract
Parkinson’s disease (PD) is normally diagnosed in a person after a thorough physical examination by a doctor that considers the patient’s medical history, neurological examination, evaluation of motor symptoms, and other supporting tests with precise diagnostic criteria. However, there is no surefire way to identify PD. Furthermore, other medical problems, including arthritis and stroke, should be evaluated on subsequent visits because they can show similarly to Parkinson’s disease. Only 80.6% of PD diagnoses are correct overall. Machine learning techniques can be applied in a variety of ways to develop different methods to recognize the presence of PD in an individual, as well as identify it in the initial stages of the disease, to aid doctors in the diagnosis of PD. The primary objective of this SLR is to explore the different methods in which features and machine learning systems can be combined to detect individuals with Parkinson’s disease (PD). We gathered, summarized, and analyzed research publications on PD detection using voice and speech datasets (and closely related topics) published between the years 2016 and 2022 for this SLR. To search for all the publications related to the issue, articles were gathered by executing a keyword-based automated search using ScienceDirect and PubMed.
Keywords: Parkinson’s disease, Bradykinesia, Ysphonia, gait, sleep disturbance, dementia, constipation, Braak model

3.1 Introduction
A neurodegenerative condition called Parkinson’s disease [1] is characterized by a progressive depletion of dopamine in the brain. The substantia nigra region of the brain is affected by this age-related illness. This region has a midbrain dopaminergic nucleus, plays a vital role in regulating motor activity and reward functions. It is a crucial brain region for the generation of dopamine. The manifestation of PD symptoms occurs when there is a significant loss of dopaminergic neurons or an alteration in the amount of dopamine in the brain.
As one ages, the number of connections between brain cells decreases, and the neurons shrink in size. Unlike cells found in muscles, skin, and bones, nerve cells lack the ability to regenerate themselves. As we age, our neurons deteriorate or die. Parkinson’s disease is linked to both motor and non-motor characteristics, including the ability to plan, initiate, and complete movements. Motor symptoms, which include Bradykinesia (slowness in walking), Ysphonia (changes in speech), gait (short steps), and stiff muscles, are those that affect how the body moves. Sleep disturbance, loss of smell, dementia, constipation, hallucinations, and other nonmotor symptoms are a few examples.
Parkinson’s disease cannot be prevented because the cause of the death of dopamine-producing neurons is still unclear, but the condition can be caught early and treated appropriately to decrease the disease’s course. Parkinson’s disease patients receive treatment with drugs like syndopa and levodopa, the dosage of which depends on how severe the condition is.
Parkinson’s disease affects over 1% of individuals who are 65 years or older [1]. Ninety percent of persons with PD report speech impairment [2, 3], and A significant number of individuals exhibit speech-related symptoms during the initial stages of the condition [4]. Symptoms of PD include reduced inflection in pitch or loudness for expressing emotion or meaning, decreased intensity, altered voice quality, and a limited range of articulatory movements. Repeated syllables can cause a decline in volume and articulation. Rush (words that are spoken quickly) and dysfluency (caused by hesitations or a loss of rhythm) make comprehension even more difficult. This collection of flaws is often referred to as hypokinetic dysarthria [5]. Its manifestation is somewhat dependent on internal cues and sensory processing, similar to bradykinesia [6, 7]. Although PD patients talk softly, they mistakenly think their speech is louder than it actually is [8]. Most aspects of parkinsonian dysarthria improve in response to outside signals [9]. Apart from the aforementioned conditions, voice patterns can also be affected by autism, dyslexia, heart disease, and Alzheimer’s disease [10–12].
As per the Braak model that describes the progression of a PD in a pathological manner, Lewy (alpha-synuclein) pathology first affects the medulla oblongata and pons [13]. In Braak stages 1 and 2 of PD, speech impairment may manifest before the appearance of additional motor symptoms [14, 15]. The prosody of speech was recently shown to be impacted in people with solitary REM sleep behavior disorder and decreased olfaction, which are premonitory nonmotor deficits [16]. Perceptual speech analysis contributes significantly to our present understanding of parkinsonian speech problems. The subjective approach suffers from several drawbacks, such as inherent variations in people’s voices, external cues effects, and context-related variations in verbal communication. In order to address these challenges, computerized speech or voice analysis has been suggested as a potential method for the diagnosis and monitoring of PD.
This review seeks to fill the gaps between these several fields, advance technological knowledge, and specify clinical needs. Our literature review began in the year 2016 as the starting point since our preliminary reading indicated that the pace of research in this area has increased since that year [17, 18]. The aim of our literature review was to establish connections among different studies on speech and voice in PD and determine the acoustic features and phonemes that best indicate the disease’s seriousness.

3.2 Research Questions
Research questions serve the purpose of clarifying the objectives of a systematic review, enabling the readers to comprehend what the review aims to achieve. The questions are devised to encompass all the essential concepts and significant aspects relevant to detecting PD. The aim is to explore the literature, analyze and consolidate the accessible data on research topics, types, methodologies, contributions, and gaps, from the viewpoint of scholars and practitioners, in the context of PD detection. The research questions that were given priority include:

What are the individual stages that make up the process of detecting PD?
What are the different datasets based on the voice that can be utilized to train models for the identification of PD?
What are the different voice recording characteristics that can be extracted and utilized for training models to detect PD in individuals?
What ML/Non-ML algorithms have been used for PD detection?
What are the accuracy metrics used?

3.3 Method
The systematic review followed the PRISMA guidelines. All papers were collectively evaluated to identify those that were not suitable for further assessment based on three criteria:

insufficient statistical analysis;
poor data analysis; and
inadequate methodological details.

The selected publications were analyzed to assess both the technical and medical aspects, as well as the quality of the methodology and the reliability of the results. Additionally, papers that did not meet the criteria for statistical analysis, data analysis, or methodological detail were excluded from further assessment.

3.3.1 Search Strategy
The study utilized the combination of search terms “Parkinson’s disease,” “speech,” “voice,” and “machine learning” to search two databases, ScienceDirect and PubMed, for publications between 2016 and 2022. A total of 220 papers were screened based on the title and abstract, and 60 papers were selected for full-text reading.

3.3.2 Inclusion Criteria
Several studies have been selected or rejected for this systematic literature review (SLR) based on specific parameters. Inclusion and exclusion criteria were used to determine which studies met the requirements for this review. The inclusion criteria for a study refer to the specific characteristics that it must possess to be considered for the review and this is shown below in Figure 3.1 that represent the flow diagram of PRISMA. The following inclusion criteria were considered for this SLR:

Figure 3.1 The flow diagram of PRISMA for literature search and selection process illustrates the number of research papers that were found, filtered, and included in the SLR.

Research papers published between 2016 and 2022 that explored the application of speech and/or voice in detection or monitoring PD were included in this study.
Differentiating between individuals with PD and healthy controls (HC) based on classification.
Differentiating PD from other syndromes of parkinsonism.

Studies that fell into one or more of the following groups were not included:

studies on Parkinsonism or associated illnesses that did not involve PD classification or detection;
studies which involved PD detection using other datasets other than voice and speech datasets were excluded;
studies that have nothing to do with PD diagnosis;
study did not employ any metrics to assess categorization performance;
study’s descriptions of the machine learning techniques, data sets, and subjects used were insufficient or inaccurate;
not original documents for a journal article or a conference; and
article not in English.

3.3.3 Subprocesses Involved in PD Detection Process
Typically, the PD detection process encompasses the subprocesses of preprocessing of the dataset, feature extraction process, feature selection process, and classification of selected features.

3.3.4 Data Sets
Following are the voice and speech recording datasets used by different researchers for PD detection:

3.3.4.1 Parkinson’s Data Set—UCI Machine Learning Dataset
Max Little, in association with the National Center for Speech and Speech in Denver, Colorado, recorded speech signals to create this dataset [22]. It comprises biomedical voice measurements from 31 individuals, of which 23 have PD. Each row of the table represents one of these individuals’ 195 audio recordings, and each column is a specific audio measurement [22]. The dataset aims to distinguish healthy people from those with PD, with the “status” column setting healthy individuals to 0 and PD patients to 1. Of the 23 attributes in the dataset, 21 are acoustic features.

3.3.4.2 PC-GITA Dataset
The dataset comprises voice recordings of 50 individuals diagnosed with PD and their age and gender-matched HC who are all Spanish native speakers [23]. The recordings were taken using a rule that adhered to technical requirements and recommendations from experts in the fields of linguistics, phonetics, and neurology. The tasks included in this dataset cover various aspects of speech, such as sustained vowel vocalization, diadocokinetic assessment, 45 words, 10 sentences, reading texts, and monologs.

3.3.4.3 mPower Dataset
The mPower project, which aimed to evaluate innovative metrics to complement traditional behavioral symptom assessments for tracking Parkinson’s disease progression and diagnosis, generated this dataset through a mobile application-based study [23]. This study seeks to comprehend the sources of, frequency of, and degree of variation in patient symptoms, as well as any potential moderators of such differences. This dataset contains data from seven different modules:

Demographics: Participants in this survey replied to inquiries about their health history and general demographic information [21].
MDS-UPDRS: Selected questions from the UPDRS scale of the Movement Disorder Society were answered by survey respondents [21].
PDQ-8: The PD questionnaire short form was used for participants to respond to in this survey.
Memory: Participants engage in a brief visuospatial game associated with the Corsi block-tapping test in this module [21].
Tapping: In this module, participants perform multiple taps on their mobile’s screen.
Voice: In this module, participants initially capture the surrounding noise level for 5 seconds, and upon its acceptability, they proceed to record their voice saying “aaah” for 10 seconds [21].
Walking: Participants are instructed to walk back and forth for a duration of 20 to 30 seconds while keeping their mobile in their pocket in this module [21]. They are subsequently asked to stand still for another 20 to 30 seconds.

3.3.4.4 Mobile Device Voice Recordings (MDVR-KCL) Dataset
The dataset was captured at the KCL Hospital in Denmark Hill, Brixton, London, where a standard examination room measuring approximately ten square meters with a typical reverberation time of 500 milliseconds was employed for speech recordings [19]. Motorola Moto G4 smartphones were used to record the data. The dataset includes voice recordings collected from 16 individuals diagnosed with PD and 21 healthy individuals [19]. The first task involved reading a paragraph of text, and the second task involved engaging in spontaneous conversation, both of which were completed by all 37 participants. The recordings are approximately 150 seconds long and are saved in the WAV format.

3.3.4.5 Italian Parkinson’s Voice and Speech (IPVS) Dataset
The Italian Parkinson’s Voice and Speech dataset is a collection of audio recordings of individuals with Parkinson’s disease speaking in Italian [20]. The dataset was created to support research in the area of voice and speech analysis for the early detection and monitoring of Parkinson’s disease.
The dataset contains audio recordings from 28 individuals with Parkinson’s disease and 20 healthy individuals serving as controls. Each participant was asked to read a set of standardized sentences in Italian, resulting in a total of 2,000 utterances in the dataset. The recordings were made in a soundproof room using a high-quality microphone and were stored in WAV format.
The dataset also includes various measures of speech quality and voice characteristics, such as the fundamental frequency, jitter, shimmer, and harmonics-to-noise ratio. These measures can be used to evaluate the severity of Parkinson’s disease and track changes in speech quality over time.

3.3.4.6 Parkinson Speech Dataset With Multiple Types of Sound Recordings Dataset
The Parkinson Speech Dataset with Multiple Types of Sound Recordings is a collection of audio recordings of individuals with Parkinson’s disease and healthy controls [24]. The dataset was created to support research in the area of speech analysis for the detection and monitoring of Parkinson’s disease. The dataset contains several types of sound recordings, including sustained phonation, vowel prolongation, and reading tasks. The recordings were made using several devices, including smartphones, tablets, and professional microphones. The dataset includes a total of 756 recordings from 42 individuals, including 23 individuals with Parkinson’s disease and 19 healthy controls.
In addition to the audio recordings, the dataset also includes various measures of speech quality and voice characteristics, such as jitter, shimmer, fundamental frequency, and harmonics-to-noise ratio. These measures can be used to evaluate the severity of Parkinson’s disease and track changes in speech quality over time and below Table 3.1 shows the Comparison of Parkinson’s voice datasets. 3.3.4.7 Parkinson’s Telemonitoring Dataset
The Parkinson’s Telemonitoring dataset is a collection of data that was collected from individuals with Parkinson’s disease using a telemonitoring system [22]. The dataset was created to support research in the area of remote monitoring of Parkinson’s disease symptoms. The dataset contains data from 42 individuals with Parkinson’s disease who were monitored remotely for up to 6 months. The data include various measures of motor symptoms, such as tremors, bradykinesia, and dyskinesia, as well as medication usage and side effects. The telemonitoring system used to collect the data included a smartphone application and a wearable device that recorded accelerometer and gyroscope data. The data were collected in real time and transmitted to a secure server for analysis.
The Parkinson’s telemonitoring dataset is a valuable resource for researchers and clinicians working in the area of remote monitoring of Parkinson’s disease symptoms. It is publicly available for research purposes and can be downloaded from the Parkinson’s Disease Digital Biomarker DREAM Challenge website.

3.4 Algorithms
Good volume of relevant work had been done in relation to Parkinson Disease detection and diagnosis. The field is increasingly adopting Machine Learning due to its ability to recognize patterns.
Author—Anurag Jain, Rohit Lamba, Tarun Gulati, and Pooja Rani Paper—“A Speech-Based Hybrid Decision Support System for Early Detection of Parkinson’s Disease” [74]
In this study, two different speech datasets were utilized—the IPVS data-set consisting of 28 individuals with PD and 20 healthy peoples (HC), and the MDVR-KCL dataset comprising of 16 PD and 21 healthy individuals HC. Both datasets contain voice recordings in “.wav” file format. Feature extraction was performed using the Parselmouth Python library, resulting in the extraction of 17 acoustic features. The resultant table matrix comprises of 19 columns, including a voice ID column, 17 feature columns, and a status column. To identify the most distinguishing features, two different techniques genetic algorithm and extra tree were utilized. The extra tree technique identified 10 features from the 17 available, while the genetic algorithm method identified 8 features. The extra tree method utilized 10 estimators, while the genetic algorithm method utilized 18 generations with a population size of 100 in each individual generation. The next generation was created by combining 20 randomly selected chromosomes and 20 best chromosomes, with 0.5 as crossover rate. A mutation rate of 0.05 was used to bring diversity in the population. To detect PD, four different classifiers were used: (i) K-NN, (ii) XGBoost, (iii) RF, and (iv) LR.
Author—Chethan C R, Divitha D, Abhishek M S, Aditya C R, Nagaraju T R, Paper—“Diagnosis of Parkinson Disorder through Speech Data” [75]
In this study 13 different acoustic features were obtained from each captured voice signal. In this study, a function was developed that takes multiple intricate measurements on an input audio file. These measurements were then transferred to the Parselmouth library, which implements Praat within the Python code. They have used MDVR-KCL dataset. They have used K Nearest Neighbour classifier to classify PD from healthy individuals and has reached the accuracy of 85% considering all features and in case only optimized features were used the accuracy were 98%, 94%, and 93%.
Author—Mehmet Bilal Er, Ibrahim Isik, Esme Isik Paper—“Parkinson’s detection based on combined CNN and LSTM using enhanced speech signals with Variational mode decomposition” [79]
This study has proposed a speech signals based model, which was based on deep learning to identify PD. They have used PC-GITA dataset. They have applied noise reduction using the variational mode decomposition method to produce images from voice samples. Three networks, ResNet-18, 50, and 101, were put to use to extract different features, and to classify these features LSTM model is used. The parameters used to measure performance were precision, accuracy and sensitivity, and 98.61% accuracy was obtained.
Author—Sruthi Kurada, Abhinav Kurada
Paper—“VGGish Embeddings Based Audio Classifiers to Improve Parkinson’s Disease Diagnosis” [65]
The outcomes of five distinct feature extraction techniques are compared in this study. King’s College London’s Mobile Device Voice Recordings dataset provided the audio data for this study (MDVR-KCL). The five feature extraction techniques used in our study are: VG—Gish embeddings, ResNet50-based extraction, mel-frequency cepstral coefficient (MFCC)-based filter banks, a dysphonia-specific handpicked feature set extracted through PRAAT, and a larger handpicked set extracted through the DisVoice framework. The YouTube 8M dataset’s substantial number of audio samples served as the pre-training data for the 11-layer CNN VGGish model. This model is modified from the VGG16 architecture to accommodate log mel spectrogram-based inputs for feature extraction. An evenly dispersed training set that contained 80% of the voice clips was used to train each model. The hyperparameters were adjusted using fivefold cross-validation process on the training set. The clips in the training set for each patient belonged to only one fold in order to prevent the revealing of patient-specific information. A held-out set comprising 20% of the data was used to evaluate performance.
Author—Hakan Gunduz
Paper—“Deep Learning-Based Parkinson’s Disease Classification Using Vocal Feature Sets” [66]
By applying filters of various sizes to layer-wise inputs of CNN, the Convolution is performed. The activation function is applied to the results of the convolution layers. The quantity of connections between the successive layers is the primary distinction between ANN and CNN. Additionally, CNN filters transform the receptive field data to a high-level feature representation on deeper layers.
Here authors have created multiple categorization frameworks with various input layers in accordance with feature combination strategies. One input layer, six convolution layers, one fully linked (dense) layer, and one output layer make up our first framework, a nine-layered CNN. In this framework, before sending various feature sets to input layer of network they are combined. Parallel layers are used in the second framework. Two convolutional layers make up each branch of the parallel layer, which is utilized to sort out the deep features from each feature set independently. Using the KERAS software and predefined hyperparameters, they have train their CNN classifiers. The first method to stop overfitting in training is the L2 regularizer. Another method for addressing the issue of over- fitting is dropout. Given that CNN takes into account the spatial link between nearby features, we must rearrange the features in the input data for a better prediction of relationships between the features.
SVM Support Vector Machines is a ML model that comes under the category of supervised learning and is used for classification and regression problems. When dealing with dichotomous classification tasks, if the data exhibit linearity, discrimination can be achieved using numerous hyperplanes. SVM, through kernel techniques, can effectively perform nonlinear categorization. For example, the fitted model complexity can be defined by taking C as the regularization parameter.
Author—Arif Gulten, Akin Ozcift
Paper—“Classifier ensemble construction with rotation forest to improve medical diagnosis performance of machine learning algorithms” [34]
In this particular study, an evaluation is provided that can aid in the CFS algorithm’s ability to minimize the feature dimensions of three datasets used for benchmarking purposes. To measure the accuracy of algorithms employed in the diagnosis of Parkinson’s, diabetes, and heart disorders, 30 base and ensembles of RF classifiers are used. Leave-one-out is used to validate every experiment (10-fold cross validation). with enhanced precision. The goodness of features in a classification issue can be described as follows from the perspective of correlation: A feature is typically considered to be good if it associates strongly to the class but not correlated with any other features. In order to lower the risk of Parkinson’s, heart, and diabetes datasets, they have employed the best-first search strategy in the WEKA machine learning tool to implement the CFS algorithm.
Single base classifier is less accurate than Classifier ensembles. Like Random Forest, RF consists of independent decision trees. When building an algorithm, classifiers employ hyperplanes that are parallel to the axis of features, and slight rotations of these axis leads to different trees. Finding the principle components of each K-subset using principal component analysis (PCA) is supposed to prevent the variability of information in the data. For the base classifier, new features are produced using K axis rotations. This method’s rotation strategy provides the ensemble with precision and variety. Decision trees are used for the rotation job in the classic Rotation Forest algorithm because of their sensitivity to feature axes rotation.

3.5 Features
This section briefly describes several features that are employed for voice-based Parkinson’s diagnosis.

3.5.1 Acoustic Features

3.5.1.1 Jitter (Local, Absolute)
This feature also goes by the name of jitta, the value is determined by computing the mean of the absolute (i.e, nonrelative) difference between consecutive intervals. The benchmark value of jitta in detection of pathologies in adults is evaluated as 83.2 µs.
(3.1)

3.5.1.2 Jitter (Local)
Also known as jitt, it is the average of the absolute (i.e., nonrelative) difference between consecutive intervals which is then divided according to the average interval. In order to identify pathologies, a threshold value of 1.04% is used for jitt.
(3.2)

3.5.1.3 Jitter (rap)
It shows the mean disturbance, i.e., the mean absolute difference between one interval and the mean of the interval and its two neighbors, divided according to the average interval. In order to identify pathologies, a threshold value of 0.68% is used for jitter.
(3.3)

3.5.1.4 Jitter (ppq5)
The disturbance ratio is shown by this feature within 5 intervals, i.e, it is estimated by taking the mean (average) absolute difference between the chosen period and its 4 closest neighbors.
(3.4)

3.5.1.5 Shimmer (Local)
The value obtained by dividing the mean amplitude difference between consecutive periods by mean amplitude is called Shim. In order to identify pathologies, a threshold value of 3.81% is used for Shim.
(3.5)

3.5.1.6 Shimmer (local, dB)
ShdB is the mean of the absolute variation in the base 10 logarithm of the disparity between successive periods. To identify pathologies, ShdB has a threshold value of 0.350 dB.
(3.6)

3.5.1.7 Shimmer (apq3)
A metric can be obtained by dividing the average variation in amplitude between a period and the mean amplitude of its adjacent periods by the mean amplitude. This metric represents the average of the absolute difference.
(3.7)

3.5.1.8 Shimmer (apq5)
The value is obtained by dividing the mean absolute deviation between the amplitude of a period and the average amplitude of its four closest neighbors by the mean amplitude.
(3.8)

3.5.2 Spectogram-Based Methods

3.5.2.1 MFCC
MFCC are cepstral coefficients derived on a the mel scale, which is a twisted frequency scale that focuses on human auditory perception. To calculate MFCC from audio the following steps are employeed:

Perform a Fourier transform on a signal (a windowed segment of the signal may be used).
Use triangular overlapping windows or cosine overlapping windows to map the spectrum powers onto the mel scale.
Logarithmically transform the powers at each mel frequency.
Treat the mel log power list as a signal and apply a discrete cosine transform.
The amplitudes of the resulting spectrum are referred to as MFCCs.

The procedure for calculating MFCCs from a continuous speech signal is illustrated in Figure 3.2.

Figure 3.2 Block diagram of the MFCC computation process.

3.6 Conclusion
There is still much work to be done in order to detect Parkinson’s disease early and accurately. Different methods of evaluation are being used in order to try to perfect the system. These methods include coherence, relevance, feedback, and knowledge-based. Our SLR is based on the idea that a systematic review of previous work in this domain is necessary. Several different PD detection techniques have been analyzed, and Machine Learning techniques including Logistic Regression (LR), KNN algorithm, SVM, Random Forest, and Gradient Boost are found to be the most implemented detection technique.
We have come to the following conclusions based on our systematic literature review:

As many machine learning techniques are used in different research, we will be focusing on Neural Network for PD detection as it is observed that neural networks are more effective than generic machine learning techniques.
One issue is that there are very few research which focused on early-stage PD detection, so we are focusing for early-stage PD detection.
We will be using MDVR-KCL dataset, which is a voice recording dataset. This data set is recorded using mobile phone in normal room conditions; hence, we can use it for different real application, and it is also cost effective than other dataset as it does not require any special devices for recording,
Most of the research have used common features, such as acoustic features, MFCC, AWT, etc. We will be using BARC Wavelet Feature, which is a wavelet-based feature.

4Tumor Detection and Classification
Hermehar P.S. Bedi*, Sukhpreet Kaur and Saumya Rajvanshi
Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Chandigarh Engineering College - CGC, Mohali, Punjab, India

Abstract
Uncontrolled growth of abnormal tissues in the body is referred to as the tumor. Broadly, tumors can be classified into two types: benign and malignant. The World Health Organization (WHO) claims that in the year 2020, cancer alone contributed 10 million deaths, making it the leading death causing disease. National Cancer Registry Programme of Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), estimated cases of cancer in India have seen a rise from 1,392,179 in 2020 to 14,61,427 in 2022 and reported a mortality of 7,70,230 and 8,08,558 in 2020 and 2022, respectively, which is a significant increase. Till date, more than 120 types of brain tumors have been found, which have different shapes and sizes. Due to these factors, it is important to detect the tumors immediately. Various mechanical methods exist for the detection of tumors, such as magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), computed tomography (CT) scan, and positron emission tomography (PET) scan. All these scans send the waves into the patient’s body to obtain the images of the organ or body part affected. Images obtained from these scans are of Digital Imaging and Communications in Medicine (DICOM) format. Different techniques available for classification of the tumor, such as aegmentation, feature extraction, machine learning techniques, and deep learning models, have been discussed and compared in this chapter, which can help researchers in finding the optimal solution for their research in the detection and classification of different tumors and other deadly diseases. Finally, this chapter comes up with all the relevant and important literature related with tumor detection and how it can be classified using the new age computer-based technology, such as deep learning and machine learning.
Keywords: Tumors, machine learning models, deep learning models, segmentation, feature extraction, region of interest

4.1 Introduction
Cancer is the uncontrolled growth of cells, which spread over the other organs and parts of the body. Cancer can be caused almost everywhere in the body and till now there is no well-known cause of cancer. Cancers can generally be divided into two categories:

Hematologic: This is a type of cancer, which starts in the blood forming tissues such as bone marrow or in the cells of the immune systems. Examples of such cancers are leukemia, lymphoma, and multiple myeloma.
Solid tumors: This is a type of cancer of the body organs or tissues. These generally do not contain the cysts or liquid areas. Examples of such cancers are brain, breast, prostate, and lung.

According to the World Health Organization (WHO), in 2020, cancer contributed 10 million deaths, making it the leading death causing disease of 2020 [1]. National Cancer Registry Programme of Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), estimated cases of cancer in India have seen a rise from 1,392,179 in 2020 to 14,61,427 in 2022 and reported a mortality of 7,70,230 and 8,08,558 in 2020 and 2022, respectively, which is a significant increase [2].
Tumors are also like cancer, i.e., these are also the uncontrolled growth of abnormal tissues in the body. Tumors can be classified into two categories:

Benign: these are tumors, which are not cancerous in nature.
Malignant: these are tumors, which are cancerous in nature. So, malignant tumors fall under the solid tumors category of cancer. This is the most dangerous form of tumors. Malignant tumors alone contribute 14,000 deaths in a year.

Based on the severity level, tumors are classified into three different grades:

Grade 1: It is the least dangerous grade, where the tumor cells resemble the normal cells and these cells do not grow much rapidly.
Grade 2: It is the grade of tumors, where the tumor cells do not resemble the normal cells and the growing is also fast as compared to the normal cells.
Grade 3: It is the grade of tumors, where the tumor cells look abnormal and starts spreading or replicating at very fast pace.

According to a report “Cancer Statistics 2020,” which is published by American Cancer Society, approximately 24,000 people would have been affected by only one kind of tumor, the brain tumor, and it will contribute 19,000 deaths in USA alone [3].
Till today, there are more or less 120 types of brain tumors found in different shapes and sizes, which, due to the complex structure of brain, is difficult to detect [4].
Imaging plays a very crucial role in the detection and treatment of brain tumors and the factor, which has emerged as an indicator in the planning of treatment and the outcome assessment is the size of the tumor [5].

4.2 Methods Used for Detection of Tumors
There are several radiological techniques available for detecting different tumors. Radiological techniques, such as magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), computed tomography (CT), positron emission tomography (PET) scans along with the combination of these techniques, such as PET-MRI and PET-CT scans, are also used to detect the tumors. The main principle behind these scans is sending different kinds of waves (depending on the type of scan) into the patient’s body, gathering the images, and identifying the abnormalities in those images.
Most common scans used for the detection of tumors are discussed below:

MRI: The term MRI, which stands for magnetic resonance imaging, is an advanced image-based diagnosing technique used for the detection of different diseases in the body based on the frequency and intensity of the protons and their resonance. MRI works on the principal of nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR), which produces the high-quality images of the parts, instead of using uniform field, a varying magnetic field is applied across the body part. Protons of different regions come to the resonance at non-identical frequencies. Intensity of signals is proportional to the number of protons at each magnetic field. Images and data obtained from this are then used by doctors to detect the tumors. For easy and detailed reading, these images are broken into slices or small segments by the process named segmentation, which is discussed later in this chapter [6].
CT: The term CT, which stands for computed tomography, is an image-based diagnosing technique used for the different diseases in the body using a beam of X-rays. In CT scan, a narrow beam of X-Rays is aimed at a patient and then it is revolved around the body of the patient, which produces the signals that are processed and the cross-sectional images known as slices are generated by the machine [7].
PET: The term PET, which stands for positron emission tomography, is a noninvasive functional imaging method, which works at the molecular level. It gives a detailed quantitative information about the disease and is many times used to find out the cancer, infection, and inflammation by perceiving the emitted photons from a radiotracer dye, which is localized to abnormal cells. In PET scan, the patient is injected with a radioactive dye, which is referred to as the tracer or radiotracer dye, which helps in identifying the disease by highlighting the part affected [6, 8].

Presently, the process of anomaly detection through the above stated imaging techniques is performed manually in most of the cases, and the doctors spend a lot of time detecting and segmenting it. The manual techniques are prone to human error, which can sometimes cost the life of patient. To overcome this problem and save the lives of patient, researchers have shifted their focus on various machine learning (ML)– and deep learning (DL)–based techniques for automatic detection and segmentation [4], which are further discussed as we delve deeper in this chapter. In order to reduce the errors, early detection of tumors and high accuracy of the ML and DL models to predict and detect the tumors, following points are made in context to the chapter: 1) Data capturing 2) Image Pre-processing 3) Segmentation 4) Feature Extraction 5) Training the ML or DL models using suitable classification algorithms [9].

Data Capturing: The first step to detect and classify the tumor is the collection of data, in this case, the images using which the tumor could be found out. The dataset can contain different types of images, such as axial, sagittal, and coronal.
Image Pre-processing: This is the step, in which the unwanted data known as noise is removed from the image using different methods, such as mean filter, median filter, and hybrid filters. Filters are used to alter the size, shade, color and also to subdue the inflated frequencies of the image. Many times, during the pre-processing stage only the image reconstruction also takes place.
Segmentation: After the images have been processed and the noise has been removed, now the next step is to divide/slice/ split the images into smaller parts. All the pixels are assigned a tag. This slicing of the image creates a number of sets of pixels within an image. Every set of the pixels contain pixels having same tag. Similar tags of pixels denote a distinct feature [6]. There are different segmentation techniques used for segmenting the images, which are further discussed in the chapter. Figure 4.1 shows the steps for classifying the tumors.

Figure 4.1 Steps for classifying the tumors.

Feature Extraction: Extracting the features from the images is a tedious, time consuming and one of the most important tasks. Features that have been used to probe the images help people differentiate between the tumors and other tissues. Different features, such as standard deviation, kutosis, variance, etc., are extracted during this stage [6, 9].
Training the model using suitable classification algorithms: This is the step, where the model is trained using the different classification algorithms. After model training, the trained model is validated or tested. Different ML and DL algorithms are used to train the model, which are discussed and compared further in the next topics as we delve deeper into the models.

4.3 Methods Used for Classification of Tumors
For classification of tumors, different classification techniques are used such as the Segmentation, Machine Learning and Deep Learning models. To classify the tumors, the models are trained using different classification algorithms such as KNN, SVM, DT, Random Forest, XGBoost Classifier, ResNet 18, DenseNet 169, DenseNet 201 etc. Different algorithms have different accuracy of detecting and classifying the tumors.

4.3.1 Segmentation
As discussed earlier, segmentation is the technique, which is used to split the image into different smaller parts and assign the tags to pixels. Pixels having same tag have feature in common. Different segmentation techniques used to classify the tumors from images are Histogram Based, Threshold based, Region growing, K means, watershed etc.

4.3.2 Region Growing Method
It is a region-based segmentation method, which rely on the assumption that pixels within the same region have same values. Region Growing is also referred to as pixel-based image segmentation technique shown in Figure 4.2. Region Growing method inspects the adjacent or the bordering pixels of the starting seed point and decides whether the pixel should be added to the region or not. Region Growing Method comprises of three different methods, which include Seeded Region Growing Method, Unseeded Region Growing Method and λ Region Growing Method. This method correctly separates the region based on the properties of similar pixels [10].

Figure 4.2 Different types of segmentation.

4.3.3 Seeded Region Growing Method
It is one of the simplest and very alluring for semantic image segmentation. Since, it is fast and free from the tuning points, it is most frequently used or it is given the preference to be used [11]. To check whether a pixel belongs to the region or not, the difference between the intensity of the pixel and region mean is used [12]. This method performs the segmentation in the respect of a set of a point, which is known as seed. It is the choice of seeds which decides what is Region of Interest (ROI) and what is irrelevant. In this, the number of seeds are grouped into n sets let us assume them to be A1,A2,A3,…,An. Individual sets may consist of single point. From the seeds, the algorithm then finds the tessellation of the image into regions with the property all the components of the region meets with one of the Ai and based on this, the region is chosen to be homogenous. This process develops synthetically from the seeds. Each step of seeded region growing method includes the addition of one pixel to one of the above sets [13]. According to the study of Praveen Gamage, the seeded region growing method shows the highest accuracy of 92% [14].

4.3.4 Unseeded Region Growing Method
It is the upgraded variant of the Seeded Region Growing method, in which the explicit seeds are not required [6].

4.3.5 λ-Connected Method
It is drawn on the linking path and the intensity of the pixels of images under consideration. It is related to fuzzy method created by Rosenfeld who treated image as a 2 dimensional fuzzy set. A pixel set is said to be λ connected if there is a λ connected path for any two points (λ is the fuzzy value between 0 and 1). The main key on which it is based is that an object must be smooth inside and should have a gap at its boundary in terms of the intensity [15].

4.3.6 Threshold Based Method
It is one of the best and the simplest segmentation techniques available, which shows the accuracy of 91% [14]. In this type of segmentation, firstly the image is converted to binary form from the already existing greyscale image. In this, the intensity of pixels is compared to threshold intensity or the intensity, which has already been defined [6], threshold values are obtained from the histogram of the edges of the original images [16]. The value of histogram is calculated on the basis of the edge detection, which means that the value of threshold is precise only if the edge detection is errorless [17]. This technique takes as a hypothesis that all the pixels, which lies in the same range belongs to one class [18].

4.3.7 K-Means Method
This type of segmentation technique uses the raw image, that is the image, which has not been pre-processed as a point having a location in the k-space. It is an unsupervised technique, due to which it requires less human interaction. It is useful for the large images having bad or poor contrast. It is susceptible to sample selection and setting up the fuzzy sets might be difficult [19].

4.3.8 Watershed Method
This method has the ability to classify the intensities with very less differences, which is not possible by other methods such as snake and level set method [6]. This method only works on the pre- processed image. If the image is not already processed, then it will lead to over segmentation of the image. According to the study of [14], this method shows the accuracy of 85.5%, which is one of the least as compared to other segmentation techniques used by him. Table 4.1 shows the advantages and disadvantages of different segmentation techniques and Table 4.2 shows the different segmentation technique and the Table 4.3 shows the Comparison of region based and threshold based segmentation techniques.

4.4 Machine Learning
Machine learning (ML) is a process of feeding machine with sufficient data to train and predict the possible outcome using different techniques. Efficiency of the technique or algorithm depends upon the quality and usefulness of the data. There are different types of Machine learning available, which are supervised learning, unsupervised learning and reinforcement learning shown in Figure 4.3.

Figure 4.3 Types of machine learning techniques.

4.4.1 Supervised Learning
Supervised machine learning is the type of machine learning, in which the machine is supervised for learning. The most common example of this supervised learning is the classification. For supervised learning, the data provided is the labelled. Supervised learning is further divided into two categories: Classification and Regression. In the supervised learning, generally, a set of n features X1, X2,...,Xn is provided, which is measured on n observations, and a response Y also measured on those same n observations. The goal is then to predict Y using X1, X2,...,Xn.

4.4.2 Unsupervised Learning
It is the type of machine learning, in which the data is neither labelled nor classified and algorithms are used to find the pattern in such datasets. The algorithms are hence required to classify and label the data in the data-set without having any external guidance or human interference to perform the task [25]. Unsupervised learning is further divided into two sub groups, which are: Clustering and Dimensionality Reduction.

4.4.3 Reinforcement Learning
It is the type of machine learning, in which the model is rewarded if the results obtained are desired and punished, if the results obtained are undesired. Mostly, this type of learning is used in the games and very less often used in other domains such as medical diagnosis and classification.
Training and Testing are performed on the known or the unknown datasets, so that the classification approaches can be used to categorize the input data into different classes. ML is very frequently used for the classification of the tumors into different classes such as presence of tumor or not, benign or malignant [26]. Different ML methods, such as KNN, SVM, Random Forest, etc, can be used to classify tumors from the images of different radio diagnostic scans of humans. Some of the ML techniques, which are actively been used to detect and classify the tumors into the one of the above stated types are discussed below.

4.4.4 K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN)
It is one of the easiest classification algorithms, which provide the results with high accuracy. Most of the times, KNN is used for the pattern recognition. In KNN, all the available cases are stockpiled and the new cases are classified based on the similarity measure. The instance is classified on the basis of the votes received for its nearest neighbour class shown in Figure 4.4. Generally, the Euclidean formula is used to find the distance between the instance and its nearest neighbour. Sometimes, Manhattan and Minkowski formulas are also used to calculate the same.
Euclidean Formula:

Manhattan Formula:

Figure 4.4 (a) 1 nearest neighbour. (b) 2 nearest neighbours. (c) 3 nearest neighbours.
Minkowski Formula:

4.4.5 Support Vector Machine (SVM)
SVM is used to split the images into various different classes as is done by the KNN algorithm. It is also a type of classification algorithm, which classifies the given image into different classes. It uses a hyperplane that differentiates between the two given classes and then classifies the given the instance into the class. It constructs a hyperplane based out of a kernel function as shown in the graph 4.1 below, where feature vectors on the left side belong to the -1 (negative) class and the feature vectors on the right side belongs to the +1 (positive) class [27].

4.4.6 Random Forest
Random Forest is a bagging method algorithm, which uses the bootstrap average to make the predictions. Random Forest algorithm creates several different decision trees and the final prediction is made by taking into consideration the majority vote, which is finalized by analysing the predictions of all the decision trees [28].

Graph 4.1 Classification using SVM [27].

4.5 Deep Learning (DL)
Deep Learning is a very active area of research in the Machine Leaning and Artificial Intelligence communities. The key basis or the foundation of Deep learning is the Neural Networks. Neural Networks caught more fame in the late 1980’s and early 1990’s. there are different types of neural networks such as Convolutional Neural Networks for image classification and Recurrent Neural Networks for time series and other sequences [29]. Different DL methodologies are used to cram the medical data. These methodologies include CNN, encoded CNN, 3D CNN, U-Net, LSTM, RNN, which are discussed further in the chapter [26].

4.5.1 Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN)
CNN is a multi-layered neural network, which focuses to process the grid structured data. CNN basically works by combining the two specialized layers, which are the Convolution layer and the Pooling layers.

4.5.1.1 Convolution Layer
This layer performs an operation, known as convolution, which is based on the linear algebra operation, which multiplies matrix of the filter in the image to be processed [30]. The main work of this layer is to find out whether a particular feature is present in an image or not. The convolution layer is made up of large number of filters, known as convolution filters. These filters work on a simple operation, which is already described as convolution, which works by recursively multiplying matrix elements followed by the addition of the results [29]. Working of convolution filter can be understood by taking an example of two matrices one of an image and the other of the filter. Consider the image matrix of 4x3 and the convolution filter matrix of 2×2.

Resultant matrix obtained will be the convolved image, which will be smaller than the original image because its dimension is given by the number of 2×2 submatrix in the original image. The convolved image can have same dimensions as that of the original image, if we use padding.

The convolved image spotlights the region of an original image, that matches with the convolution filter.

4.5.1.2 Pooling Layer
It is the layer which takes the maximum or the average value of the pixel proportions of an image [30]. This layer provides a method to concise a larger image into smaller image. The max-pooling operation summarizes each non-duplicating 2x2 block of pixels in an image using the maximum value in the block. This reduces the size of an image and also, it provides the location invariance [29].

The max pooling matrix is obtained by collecting the maximum values of the 2x2 sub matrix.

4.5.1.3 Architecture of CNN
The convolution layer is surrounded by the kernel around the input sample to calculate different feature maps. Features identified from the input samples are represented by the small boxes on the feature map. These feature maps are then sent to the maximum collection layer. The maximum collection layer stores the relevant features and rejects the other. The features of the max-pooling layer are converted to a single dimensional (1D) vector of features in the fully connected layer, which is used to calculate the probability of the output. The architecture of the CNN is represented in Figure 4.5 [31].

Figure 4.5 Architecture of CNN [31].

4.5.1.3.1 Convolution Layer
As discussed above, it is the layer, which focuses on extracting the features by recursively multiplying matrix elements followed by the addition of the results.

4.5.1.3.2 Subsampling Layer
The main work of this layer is to reduce the size of the image data and increase the invariance of feature positions. The max pooling as discussed above divides the image into different smaller grids and then take the value which is maximum from each grid to produce the smaller image matrix, which makes it easy for the next convolution layer to process.

4.5.1.3.3 Fully Connected Layer
This layer basically changes the dimensions of the image so that it can is easy to classify it linearly. In the convolution layer, each neuron shall be converted into 1D data before assigning it to another layer, which is connected as a whole [32].
The typical architecture of the CNN for the classification of an image is shown in Figure 4.6 [29].
The main drawback of CNN is that it is inefficient for the classification of 3D MRI images. It shows less accuracy, when it comes to the classification of tumors from the 3 dimensional images of the MRI, which is still an area of improvement and has a scope of further research. To overcome this, some other methods are used, which shows good results and are further discussed in the chapter. Table 4.4 shows the Comparison of different variations of CNN models.

Figure 4.6 CNN architecture for the classification of an image showing convolution and pooling layers [29].

4.5.2 Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)
The main drawback of CNN was the low accuracy, when it comes to the classification of the images of 3D MRI. This was overcome by LSTM by providing better accuracy in the classification. The accuracy of the model for classifying glioma tumors in 3D MRI was 84%, which can be improved soon in the near future by tuning hyperparameters such as the learning rate and data augmentation for better performance [28]. LSTM is the improved version of the RNN, which suggests the memory block instead of the traditional units of RNN for solving the problems such as exploding gradient and vanishing. The main differentiating point between LSTM and RNN is that LSTM saves the long term state by adding a cell state, moreover, LSTM can link the data from the past with the information in the present [34–36]. Despite the fact that deep CNN are being implemented for the classification of brain tumors, with good accuracy, but there it was less accurate, when it came to the 3D medical data, as discussed above. To overcome this problem, a combination of CNN and LSTM was used, however that also was not highly accurate and hence remains an area of improvement in the future. LSTM performs outstandingly for the classification of 2D MRI images, when the Gaussian filters were applied and attained an accuracy of 98% [28]. Shanthi et al. [37] also proposed a hybrid of LSTM and CNN for automatic classification of brain tumors. Authors used ARO algorithm to choose the optimal value of weight in order to enhance the performance of classification. They used CNN for the generation of feature map and for the classification purposes, they used LSTM. This proposed method achieved an accuracy of 97.5%. Shtwai Alsubai et al [36] also proposed a hybrid method of CNN and LSTM, which uses CNN for feature extraction in the form of cropped images. This model achieved accuracy of 99.1%, precision of 98.8%, recall of 98.9% and F1 measure of 99%, which are one of the best achieved so far. This method is most suitable for detection from the MRI of the brain.

4.5.3 Artificial Neural Network (ANN)
ANN is a technique, which consists of a large amount of neurons that are interconnected to each other and in a way that it learns from the input. In the case of medical data, which is highly complex, this technique is highly efficient to learn and classify the data. Minakshi et al. [38] talks about a proposed method, which is a combination of fuzzy logic and ANN, which is mentioned as Adaptive Neuro-Fuzzy Inference System (ANFIS), which achieved an accuracy 98.67%, which was compared to another technique, which was a combination of DWT, PCA and KNN, which recorded an accuracy of 97.2%. The gradient in ANFIS was calculated using the back-propagation techniques, in which the losses were minimized by updating the weights and the Table 4.5 shows the Accuracy of different Models discussed above in percentage and the Table 4.6 shows the Accuracy of different techniques in Percentage.

4.6 Performance Metrics
Different evaluation metrics are used for the detection and classification challenges such as accuracy, precision, recall, specificity and F1-measure, based on which the models are assessed [42, 53].

4.6.1 Accuracy
To measure the accuracy of the models, the ratio of false positive (FP), true positive (TP), true negative (TN), and false negative (FN) is computed.
Accuracy of the model is estimated by the following formula:

4.6.2 Precision
Precision is also known as high predicted value, which is defined as the ratio of the real positives to all the positives, which includes, both true positive and false positives.
Precision of the model is estimated by the following formula:

4.6.3 Recall
Recall is generally referred to as the sensitivity or the true positive rate. It is defined as the ratio of true positives to the true positives and false negatives.

4.6.4 Specificity
Specificity is generally referred to as the True negative rate. It is defined as the ratio of true negatives to the true negatives and false positives.

4.6.5 F1-Measure
F1 score is the harmonic mean of the precision and recall.

4.7 Method Wise Trend of Using Techniques for Detection of Brain Tumor
Different ML and Deep Learning techniques, which are discussed above and some, which have not been discussed are used for the detection of brain tumor. A survey conducted by Mohammad Omid Khairandish et al. [54], have shown a graphical trend of such different techniques, which are being used by different researchers between the years 2012 and 2019, to conduct their research in the field of tumor detection. The results of the study are demonstrated in Graph 4.2.
The results of the survey shows, that CNN is the most used technique overall, followed by KNN, which contributes 13% of overall techniques, SVM, which contributes 10% and finally, the ANN, which is least used and contributes just 7% of the overall techniques used.

Graph 4.2 Technique wise contribution for the detection of brain tumor from 2012 to 2019 [54].

4.8 Conclusion
Uncontrolled growth of abnormal tissues in the body is referred to as the Tumor. Based on the severity, tumors are classified into three stages, namely stage 1, stage 2 and stage 3. According to American Cancer Society, nearly people have been affected only by Brain Tumor. Hence, it becomes very crucial to detect and classify the tumors at an early stage, for which several different techniques such as Segmentation, Machine Learning Algorithms and Deep Learning Models have been discussed. Based on the research conducted so far, it is evident that CNN and a hybrid of CNN and LSTM can be used for classifying the tumors as it shows one of the best results. The only limitation of CNN is that it cannot be relied upon for the 3 dimensional MR scans. Moreover, Seeded Region Growing Segmentation can be used for the detection of tumors as it shows one of the best results as compared to the other available segmentation techniques. Still, there is scope of further research in this area, such as improving the accuracy of hybrid technique LSTM and CNN for the classification of tumor from 3D images of MRI.
