10Diabetic Retinopathy Detection by Retinal Blood Vessel Segmentation and Classification Using Ensemble Model
Gandla Shivakanth1*, K. Aruna Bhaskar2, Bechoo Lal2, A. Shivakumar Reddy2 and D. Manasa2
1Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Koneru Lakshmaiah Education Foundation, Hyderabad, Telangana, India
2Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Koneru Lakshmaiah Education Foundation, KL University, Vijayawada, Andhra Pradesh, India

Abstract
Diabetic retinopathy is common problem to diabetic patients. According to the current statistics of world, 9% and above diabetic patients are suffering with partially or complete vision loss. Retinal blood vessels perform an imperative role in the diagnostic procedure of diabetic retinopathy. However, the segmentation of retinal vessels is critical for pathological reasoning. In this article, a novel ensemble approach was developed for accurate blood vessel segmentation and classification. The retinal images had been accumulated from Digital Retinal Images for Vessel Extraction (DRIVE) and STructured Analysis of the Retina (STARE) datasets. The ensemble approach is used to segment the retinal blood vessels using bio-inspired algorithms, Cuttlefish Algorithm (CFA) for segmentation of the fundus image. The features were then extracted from the segmented pictures using the Enhanced Local Binary Pattern (ELBP) and Inverse Difference Moment Normalized (IDMN) algorithms. The obtained feature values were lastly provided as the input for ensemble classifier, Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) and Self-Organized maps (SOMs). Classifying results are collected from both classifiers and consolidated using the voting ensemble algorithm. The ensemble voting procedure was used to compile and combine the findings. According to experimental findings, the suggested technique increases segmentation and classification accuracy by 2.6% to 4% when compared to the current system.
Keywords: Convolutional neural network, cuttlefish algorithm, convolutional neural network, self-organizing maps, voting ensemble classifier, local binary pattern, inverse difference moment normalized

10.1 Introduction
Retinal fundus images are significantly used for analysis of retina including retinal blood vessels, macula, and optic disc that helps clinicians in diagnosing numerous diseases like macular hole, diabetic retinopathy, macular degeneration, glaucoma, and epiretinal membrane [1, 2]. In addition, the retinal images have additional features, which give you a clear excessive decision visualization of blood vessels in the body [3]. Retinal vessel analysis is therefore helpful in the early identification and prevention of chronic illnesses such diabetic retinopathy, cerebrovascular, and cardiovascular disorders [4, 5]. Recently, numerous methodologies are developed for retinal vessel segmentation and classification like fully convolutional neural networks [6], extreme learning machine [7], Segmentation and Feature selection model introduced by using Cuttlefish optimizing technique [8], Cuttlefish optimizer framework [9], cascade classification framework [10], etc. The foundation of earlier approaches is supervised learning using pixel-level segmentation labels. Additionally, past approaches are divided into groups according on whether the feature descriptors were created by hand or were based on data. On the basis of the deep learning technology, a unique strategy is suggested to overcome this difficulty.
In my present work, a novel approach was proposed for enhancing the overall performance of retinal vessel segmentation and classification. Initially, the input had been accumulated from DRIVE and STARE datasets. Second step is preprocessing. Preprocessing designed with the median filter that gives a great enhancement of image contrast. Then, segmentation was carried out using ensemble machine learning model. The enhanced images are given as an input to two segmenting algorithms, first on is modified FCM, and other is SVM. The results of these two models are consolidated with classification voting ensemble process. Due to the ensemble ML model, there may be chance to get enhanced result. In modified FCM algorithm, Euclidean distance measure was replaced by Chebyshev distance measure, as it reap correct neighborhood selection to achieve accurate clusters. SVM is a supervised learning algorithm. It classifies pixels into one, two, or several groups. The process of classifying an image into one, two, or more classes is known as picture segmentation. So, SVM is a theoretical and practical standpoint, SVM has the benefit of being better at segmenting medical images and it is not sensitive to noise, over all it produce good results in segmentation, while comparing with existing systems [11]. Besides, in order to extract the feature values from the segmented retinal pictures, the ELBP and IDMN methods have been used. Using the feature level fusion approach, the extracted features have been combined. The ensemble classification was used as a starting point for the feature extraction output. In the proposed model ensemble classifier consist of two classifiers. First, one is CNN classifier and other one is random forest classifier both are used for classifying the normal and abnormal blood vessels. These two classifier results will consolidated with regression voting classifier and the predicted result will be displayed. At last, the proposed architecture overall performance will be compared with the existing systems in terms of accuracy, sensitivity, f-score, and specificity.

10.2 Literature Review
Researchers have devised a number of methods for segmenting retinal blood vessels. A survey of some significant contributions to the current literature is offered in this segment.
Swarna et al. [12] described a technique, using morphology-based global thresholding, a new approach for distilling the blood veins from retinal pictures. The centreline detection approach was also used to depict the retinal venule anatomy as well as detect capillaries. The created approach was tested using the standard fundus image datasets like DRIVE and STARE datasets. Extensive testing revealed the new strategy successfully reduces computational complexity while also drastically reducing execution time. Top researchers participated in this investigation.
Bhargava et al. [15] and Soomro et al. [14], for retinal vascular segmentation, proposed a supervised technique using fully convolutional network. For regional vessel element detection, the presented technique decreased the normal vessel segmentation issue. In addition, unsupervised post- processing was utilized to polish the final result. The experiment was done out on four datasets in this research study: High Resolution Fundus (HRF), DRIVE, STARE, and CHASE-DB1. In comparison to existing models the suggested method has a high level of robustness perspective of sensitivity, accuracy, specificity, and area under the curve. Furthermore, this technology employed was supervised learning (requiring manual modifications), which needed to be automated [16].
Bhargava et al. [17] On the basis of region growth and level set, a new retinal vascular segmentation system was introduced. The retinal pictures were initially denoised using 2D Gabor wavelet technology and a contrast constrained adaptive histogram equalization approach. In addition, the images were smoothed and the vessel boundaries were kept with the use of an anisotropic diffusion filter. Finally, retinal vessels were extracted using region-based active contour and region-growing techniques, and the findings were integrated to achieve the final segmentation. The created system’s effectiveness was average despite working with more testing set photos than training pictures.
A novel automated method for enhancing the identification blood vessel’s of fundus image is being developed to overcome these concerns.

10.3 Proposed System
In recent years, eye illness has emerged as one of the most significant causes of blindness. The majority of instances may be avoided if they are recognized early enough. The retinal blood vessel study, for example, the retinal blood vessel analysis assists the clinicians to identify diabetes patients and give appropriate treatment. The current study is working on a novel blood vessel detecting method. The suggested system in this study includes five stages: data collection, pre-processing of images, segmentation of fundus blood vessels, feature extraction, followed by classification. The workflow of the proposed system is depicted in Figure 10.1.
A. Dataset description
The retinal fundus pictures are initially gathered from two standard data-sets: STARE and DRIVE. The STARE dataset is made up of a number of retinal pictures taken using a TRC-50DX Topcon fundus camera having an image quality of 700 × 605 pixels and a 24-bit grey scale resolution. Twenty colored retinal pictures and twenty hand-labeled ground-truth photos make up STARE [16]. On the other hand, the DRIVE dataset originates from a screening program for diabetic retinopathy in the Netherlands. In this collection, the fundus photographs of 400 diabetics between the ages of 25 and 90 years are included. There are a total of 40 images, and each individual gets one at random. There are seven evidence of moderate early diabetic retinopathy in this group, whereas the remaining 33 exhibit no signs of diabetic retinopathy. The retinal pictures in the DRIVE dataset are usually in JPEG format [17]. The STARE and DRIVE dataset’s sample retinal pictures are indicated in Figure 10.2.

Figure 10.1 Process flow of proposed system.

Figure 10.2 Sample image, (a) STARE dataset, (b) DRIVE dataset.
B. Data pre-processing
The median filter is employed as a pre-processing tool once the retinal pictures have been collected. Prior to segmentation and feature analysis, there are usually several artefacts in the collected images that must be removed. The goal of the median filter is to improve the picture characteristics, as well as the qualities of medical images in order to make the best diagnosis possible. Equation is the general formula for a median filter (10.1).
(10.1)
where p(i) and q(i) are stated as two different fundus images, median p(i) and median q(i) are denoted as median value of the retinal images. Disparate little numbers among the picture pixels in the neighborhood have an effect on the median value. As a result, the median filter is particularly successful in filtering out a wide range of sounds. Compute the local contrast of the image using the Sobel filter. Compute the adaptive threshold value for each pixel using the local contrast measure and a thresholding method using Otsu’s method.
C. Segmentation
Usually, it is difficult to segment ill-defined parts in medical photos, which reduces segmentation accuracy significantly. In order to overcome this issue, segment the image into regions based on the threshold values using cuttlefish algorithm (CNF). In adaptive thresholding, the threshold value for image segmentation is dynamically adjusted for each pixel based on the local contrast of the image. This allows the algorithm to identify the boundaries more accurately between different regions in the image.
The segmented retinal fundus pictures are shown in Figure 10.3 and Figure 10.4 shows the evidence blood vessels segmented image.

Figure 10.3 Sample pre-processed image, (a) STARE dataset, (b) DRIVE dataset.

Figure 10.4 Sample blood vessel segmented image, (a) STARE dataset, (b) DRIVE dataset.
D. Feature extraction
Following image segmentation, feature extraction is carried out to get feature values from the segmented region. The process of transforming a retinal fundus image from image space to feature space and downsizing enormous quantities of redundant data is typically described as feature extraction. Meanwhile, feature extraction successfully lowers the computing complexity of the system. In this research project, feature extraction is carried out by using IDMN and ELBP to extract feature values from segmented images. The feature extraction methods are described in full below.

Inverse Difference Moment Normalized
IDMN is a mathematically defined picture texture metric that is one of the most effective (10.2). IDMN is also known as homogeneity, and it is used to compute the local homogeneity of segmented blood vessel images. IDMN also determines if a particular image is non-textured or textured by identifying the GLCM feature distribution.

E. Ensemble Classification
In this phase, using the segmented image, features extraction, and classification is performed using CNN classification and Self-Organizing Map (SOM) classifier, which identifies the normalcy and abnormalities of blood vessels and helps in diabetes diagnosis. The results of CNN and random forest classifiers are consolidated using regression voting ensemble classifier.

In this case, the weighted term, stabilizes the excessive parameters in the training set.
A self-organizing map (SOM) classifier is a type of unsupervised learning algorithm that is used for clustering and classification tasks. It is based on a neural network architecture that is designed to produce a low- dimensional representation of high-dimensional data.
The SOM algorithm works by representing the input data as a set of high-dimensional vectors, and mapping them onto a two-dimensional grid of nodes or neurons. Each neuron in the grid is associated with a weight vector, which is updated iteratively during the training process to adjust its position in the feature space.
The SOM training process involves initialization, competition, cooperation, adaptation, convergence. Once the SOM is trained, it can be used as a classifier by assigning each input data vector to the neuron with the closest weight vector. The SOM classifier can be used for various applications, including image recognition, data mining, and pattern analysis.
One of the advantages of the SOM classifier is that it can handle high- dimensional data with a relatively small number of neurons, which reduces the computational complexity of the algorithm. Additionally, the SOM can capture the underlying structure of the input data, which can be visualized in the form of a two-dimensional map or a cluster hierarchy.
The result of the CNN classifier and SOM classifier given as input to voting ensemble algorithm. A voting ensemble is a type of ensemble learning technique in which multiple models are trained on the same dataset and their predictions are combined using a voting scheme to make a final prediction. It is a popular method used in machine learning for improving the performance and robustness of models by combining the strengths of different algorithms.
The basic idea behind a voting ensemble is to train multiple models using different algorithms or configurations and combine their predictions to improve the accuracy of the final output.
F. Experimental result and discussion
This section simulates the specified setup using Python and an Intel i5 processor running at 3.5 GHz with 4 GB of RAM. The proposed system is evaluated for effectiveness on two well-known datasets: STARE and DRIVE, and its performance is compared to an existing method (Global thresholding-based segmentation [12, 13]). Specificity, f-score, sensitivity, and accuracy are used to evaluate how well the proposed system performs.

10.4 Conclusion and Future Scope
The goal of this experimental research project is to establish correct segmentation and classification methodologies to enhance the categorization of normalcy and abnormality of blood vessels, which will aid in the identification of diabetic retinopathy. The suggested approach outperformed the previous system in terms of sensitivity, f-score, accuracy, and specificity when compared to the existing system (global thresholding-based segmentation [12]). The suggested method obtained maximum accuracy in the STARE dataset and the DRIVE dataset, which is greater than the existing system. Future research can create a new unsupervised system to enhance the segmentation and classification of retinal blood vessels.

11Diabetes Prognosis Model Using Various Machine Learning Techniques
Pawan Kumar Patidar*, Manish Bhardwaj and Sumit Kumar
Department of Computer Science & Engineering Swami Keshvanand Institute of Technology, Management & Gramothan, Jaipur, India

Abstract
With the same speed that the 21st century asks of us, it is easy to believe that the fields of illnesses and their spread have expanded substantially in a world of quick paces and widespread adoption of bad health care practices. It is a collection of business intelligence (BI) tools that use cutting-edge machine learning techniques to uncover relationships and patterns in vast amounts of data. We can predict behaviors and events thanks to these data-driven linkages and patterns. Predictive analytics provides you a look into the future by using past events. It is important to note that the predictive model was built using previous predictive approaches, even if it is not based on the production of a mathematical model or algorithms for the development of the forecast. Instead, it uses algorithms that are built into the identified tool. It is suggested that, via the usage of the model, the organizations that provide both public and private health services adopt it in a commercial setting, using the model’s predictive capabilities for the client’s diagnosis and the optimization of consultation procedures.
Keywords: Diseases, diabetes, machine learning, prediction, supervised learning, unsupervised learning

11.1 Introduction
We see hope life increase and we regularly see in the news celebrations of the oldest people in the world; however, it is not something alien to us to find out about deaths close to early ages and due to factors not typical of the natural degeneration of the human body, but due to diseases to the heart, cancer, or complications derived from diabetes. It is normal for us to ignore our bodies given the pressures of family, school, and job, yet doing so may be quite damaging [1]. According to a report from the American Journal of Public Health 3, the number of people who would die from chronic illnesses will climb by 72% by 2020 (from 29 million to 50 million people). Given that chronic illnesses are a phenomenon that is fundamental to modernity and whose peak we have yet to reach, we designate them as a target with great potential for analytical projects like the one that is suggested [1, 2].

11.1.1 Disease Identification
Starting with a formal description of the idea under study is important in order to go forward with the analysis. Chronic or non-communicable illnesses are those that have a long duration, are contagious-free, and often advance slowly. They are the leading cause of mortality worldwide, accounting for 70% of all causes of death [3]. Cardiovascular diseases, diabetes, cancer, obesity, rheumatoid arthritis, and respiratory illnesses are the leading chronic diseases [4]. It becomes necessary to decide which illnesses will serve as the primary focus of research when there are a representative number of them. The monetary criteria will be used first because of the substantial economic relevance associated with chronic illnesses and their treatment [4].
According to the costs associated with the four primary chronic illnesses in the US, we may identify them by economic quantification as follows:

Cardiovascular Conditions
Diabetes
Cancer
Obesity

These four categories will be used as a starting point to conduct the following study about their description and symptoms since they have the biggest economic effect [4, 5].
a) Cardiovascular Diseases
Heart attacks, strokes, coronary heart disease, and other conditions that affect the heart and blood vessels are included in the category of cardiovascular illnesses:

Cerebrovascular diseases: conditions affecting the brain’s blood vessels.
Diseases of the blood arteries supplying the upper and lower limbs are known as peripheral arthropathies.
Rheumatic heart disease: heart and heart valve lesions brought on by rheumatic fever, a condition brought on by streptococcus bacteria.
Congenital heart disease: cardiac defects that exist from birth.
Deep vein thromboses (thrombi) and pulmonary embolisms (emboli) are blood clots that may form in the veins of the legs and travel to the lungs or heart [6].
Heart attacks and cerebrovascular accidents (CVA): these are often severe conditions brought on by blockages that prevent blood from reaching the heart or brain, respectively

b) Diabetes
Type 2 diabetes, previously known as adult-onset diabetes, is a chronic illness that arises when the body’s ability to use insulin properly or when the pancreas is unable to produce enough of it. A hormone called insulin aids in maintaining healthy blood sugar levels [7]. Without consistent care, uncontrolled diabetes will lead to hyperglycemia, which in turn causes a number of system problems, especially in the nervous and circulatory systems.
Juvenile, insulin-dependent, or childhood onset diabetes are other names for type 1 diabetes. This happens because people with this illness need daily injections of the hormone since their bodies are unable to create enough insulin on their own. Type 1 diabetes cannot be prevented by current knowledge, despite the fact that its exact etiology is still unknown [8].
Type 2 diabetes: insulin-independent diabetes, which is characterized by ineffective use of insulin, is sometimes used to describe non-diabetic and adult-onset type 1 diabetes. Almost 90% of instances of diabetes worldwide are of type 2, making it the most prevalent kind. This kind of diabetes was only recently seen in adults, but it is now also common in children [9].
Pregnancy-related hyperglycemia (increased blood sugar) that develops and rises to levels above normal but below those required to diagnose diabetes is known as gestational diabetes.
c) Cancer
Malignant tumors or malignant neoplasms are cancers that attack any area of the body. Cancer is characterized by the fast growth of malignant or aberrant cells, which reach a point of infiltrating nearby tissues or spreading to other organs [10].
According to the number of fatalities from cancer globally, the primary forms are:

Tobacco use (1.59 million)
Hepatitis (745,00)
Gastritis (723,00)Fourth, colorectal cancer (694 000)521 000 cases of breast cancer
Esophageal cancer (400,000)

d) Obesity
Overweightness is a condition in which extra adipose or fat tissue accumulates, becoming harmful to health. The BMI, which is widely used to detect obesity, is determined by dividing a person’s weight in kilograms by his or her height in meters squared. An adult with a BMI of 30 or above is considered obese [11]. Experts warn that its most negative effects act as an agent that determines and aggravates serious diseases, such as diabetes, some types of cancer, hypertension, and cardiovascular complications in the short term. From these four non-communicable diseases, the analysis is carried out for the choice of the disease that is used for the predictive model for the diagnosis of the disease.

11.1.2 Data, Information, and Knowledge
Data are a representation of facts, concepts, or instructions that have been formulated and codified for communication, interpretation, or processing by people or automated processes. Data, when used in isolation, are completely worthless. It must be modified into a form that is useful and put in context to be of any use. Data have to be changed in order to convey thoughts or expertise. Knowledge [12] is the information obtained, incorporated into the reasoning resources of an agent and ready to be used in a decision process; is the result of a learning process. By appending the previous concepts with the developed project, we can identify the data as that information that enters the developed predictive model in a timely manner and that serves to feed it: patient-specific data, such as height, weight, hemoglobin levels, etc. Information is what is obtained from the transformation of data through a series of logical relationships, which in the case of the project would be the consolidation of the patient’s traits, being necessary to emphasize that in the creation of information it is not obtained directly knowledge, but it is necessary for it to go through a series of resources that allow reasoning and learning from it, thus getting to know what the information provides based on the data entered. In this sense, there is a similarity between the concept of knowledge and the result that the model shows due to the fact that processing is used through algorithms to obtain learning [13].

11.1.3 Knowledge Discovery in Databases
The process of extracting previously unknown, implicit, and possibly valuable information from a dataset is referred to as knowledge discovery shows in Figure 11.1. A pattern in language L has been established for a data set F of F based on a data set Fs of F, a measure of confidence C, and a set of rules R, such that L represents the facts that are simpler than the enumeration of all of the facts in Fs. The knowledge found in database-monitoring software may be defined as a collection of facts uncovered by the program [14]. The power of databases to find knowledge is the grouping of all processes, automated and non-automated, that allow the exploration of databases, large and small, to extract potential knowledge. The ability to discover knowledge in databases, using current technologies currently offers new possibilities to the vast majority of industries to be applied correctly. However, it is necessary to indicate that it is essential to identify which techniques are the ones that must be applied to carry it out, because each one presents a series of advantages that make them more suitable for certain business cases. For the purposes of the project, using the premise of immediate prediction, the concept of predictive analytics will be developed [15].

Figure 11.1 Knowledge discovery process.

11.1.4 Predictive Analytics
Predictive analytics is a business intelligence suite that reveals patterns and connections in huge quantities of data, allowing organizations to forecast their future outcomes. Predictive analytics is different from other business intelligence technologies in that it provides a glimpse into the future, by utilizing previous occurrences to make an informed prediction. While some techniques seek to develop a logical forecast, such as linear regression, others, such as predictive analytics, are geared at making an empirical prediction, as opposed to just deriving a prediction based on theory.
Failure to appreciate the basic limits of predictive analytics may lead to bad results. Without a training dataset of sufficient quantity and quality, progress will be impossible. Also, the second part is essential since it is important to define clearly the idea to be forecasted and to have past instances of the same example.

11.1.5 Supervised Learning and Machine Learning
An algorithm that uses supervised learning goes through training samples to get a classification. Predictions based on the categorization may be made in test instances. The supervised learning in context, the term machine learning or machine learning was born, which refers to the automatic detection of significant patterns in data. A characteristic of the application of machine learning is that it is applied in cases where the patterns to be identified are of high complexity, so that a human being does not have enough expertise to learn from this in a specific way [16].

11.1.6 Predictive Models
A predictive model is a common term for a set of mathematical techniques with the goal of predicting future values of a given objective, response, or dependent variable and many predictive factors and independent variables. It is preferable to account for the inaccuracy of connections in prediction since relationships are seldom perfect in reality. In order to identify those factors that intervene in the prediction, they may be divided into three categories: those factors that have little chance of affecting the outcome, those with high certainty to influence the results, and those in the middle [17].

11.1.7 Data Validation and Cleaning
The validation of data implies determining that it is a trustworthy source of information, while the correction of mistakes in the data is referred to as data cleaning. Identifying incorrect values is common, yet locating the right values is impossible. Although it is feasible to establish a degree of confidence as to whether a given piece of data is susceptible to being accurate or wrong, a set of tests may be used to do so. You should spend more time validating your data rather than repairing the same if your data is very error-prone. Two distinct pieces of data should not be separated due to referencing the same object, this being something everyday if it is a set of information from different sources [18]. Likewise, it is not unusual that, depending on the algorithm used to make the prediction, a transformation of the variables must be made to discrete values or to binary values that are supported by the algorithm. It is everyday to have human intervention in this phase due to the complexity of the information with which you can work.

11.1.8 Discretization
Numerical qualities may be discretized, as can the selection of features. The classifiers are split into two main groups: supervised classifiers and unsupervised classifiers. In supervised discretization, if the class of the instances is a decision attribute, and if the class is not regarded or handled as a regular attribute, unsupervised discretization is used. The discretization techniques may be categorized according to three distinct axes: Global (compared to local) v/s local (unsupervised to supervised) shown in Figure 11.2, and static (as opposed to dynamic).

Figure 11.2 Supervised vs unsupervised learning.
Chronic Diseases: Chronic illnesses and diseases that are not infectious are often referred to as non-communicable diseases (NCDs). Stroke, heart attack, diabetes, cancer, asthma, and depression are all examples of medical conditions that may be included in common. Many of the risk factors for developing non-communicable diseases are preceded by unhealthy behaviors, and later by the emergence of metabolic and illness risk factors. In order to successfully manage chronic illnesses, the risk factors linked with obesity and inactivity, high blood pressure, elevated blood glucose levels, and inadequate cholesterol levels must be controlled. This list of risk factors is made up of two components: behaviors and medicines. Reducing risk factors for chronic illnesses is possible by eating a nutritious diet.
Diabetes: The set of metabolic disorders with hyperglycemia as a consequence of insulin insufficiency, insulin insufficiency itself, or both is called diabetes mellitus. The long-term damage, malfunction, and failure of numerous organs, particularly the eyes, kidneys, nerves, heart, and blood arteries, are often seen in diabetes, especially type 1 diabetes. Diabetes is usually diagnosed into two etiologies, with most patients falling into one of these two groups. The primary cause of type 1 diabetes is an utter lack of insulin secretion. Type 2 diabetes occurs more often than type 1 diabetes, although in this latter condition, the two root causes are related: a resistance to the action of insulin and an insufficient secretory response to insulin compensation. Type 2 diabetes, in which the blood glucose level is abnormally high without significant symptoms, occurs when there is sufficient hyperglycemia to induce structural and functional abnormalities in various tissues, but before the condition is recognized.

11.2 Literature Review
According to a review of the literature, several research using varied approaches and producing a range of results have looked at the prediction of diabetes. The capacity for making precise predictions about future data using models that can be developed and tested is known as predictive capability.
In [19] predictive capacity is defined a collection of advanced statistical models and estimations are used to find out what the environment will do next future. An effective way to enable consistency and predictability in reports and give users the best options is by using a predictive analytics platform that includes data warehouses, predictive analytics methods (like regression analysis, machine learning, and neural networks), dashboards, and data visualization.

11.2.1 Neural Networks
There are things to be done to make the predictions in these studies of (about) neural network algorithms more accurate [20]. A research that used various supervised learning algorithms of artificial neural network looked at a model of diabetes that made predictions. The scaled conjugate gradient method yielded superior outcomes when the objectives and expectations for results were highly correlated [21]. It describes the use of a medical diagnostic assistance system as being critical for physicians in the process of making a diagnosis. Because they wanted to conduct a research to compare many predictive models of neural network algorithms for the diagnosis of diabetes, they performed a study of several predictive models of neural network algorithms. The accuracy may be enhanced by pre-processing and extracting certain model features. In [22], we find that all diabetes prediction models are flawed, therefore we propose using fuzzy logic neural network techniques and case-based reasoning as a diagnostic method to help avoid diabetes. A novel design that is affected by patient-related input factors helped diagnose diabetes.

11.2.2 Trees
Diabetes has recently been on the rise throughout the world, according to the Lekha [23] research linked to tree algorithms. As chronic illnesses increase, a higher degree of control is required. In this model, the authors present a model that makes use of several decision tree methods to create a Chronic Disease Diagnosis (CDD). In contrast, studies indicate that diabetes not only creates an increased chance of developing other illnesses, but is a disease that itself produces such diseases. Therefore, analytic research on diabetes is a vital endeavor. Their proposal, therefore, is to use the J48 algorithm to diagnose diabetes and improve it. To obtain an accuracy of 99.87%, they adjusted the algorithm. Rocha et al. [24] say that diabetes is on the rise in the globe, and this means that by 2025 there will be 380 million individuals with diabetes, therefore necessitating quicker and more efficient diagnoses. Finally, in Chen et al. [25], it was noted that the abundant quantity of health-related information may be used to aid prediction, prevention, and personalization of medicine, which may be achieved via the development of a framework that enables scalable predictive analysis using visual tools. Evaluation of different prediction models uses parameter optimization and algorithm performance in random forest.

11.2.3 K-Nearest Neighbors
Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM) may be predicted by factors present at birth, according to a research published in the journal [26]. In this study, the researchers evaluate the different methods and determine that the best is K-nearest neighbors. Using several statistical methods, they produce a prediction model which has an error rate that is greater than what was anticipated.
Bayesian Networks: Diabetes is listed in Fitriyani et al. [27] as the chronic illness posing the biggest threat to global health. Diabetes is predicted using a Bayesian network model with an accuracy rate of 99.51%. On the other hand, in Rashtian et al. [28], they mention that the manual analysis of information in these times has become useless with the large amount of information, so a computerized analysis is necessary. In such a way, they develop a model for the diagnosis of diabetes. This system employs Bayesian network techniques, multilayer perception, and J48 to create percepts for brands. For each method, getting various precision outcomes, it is of course Bayesian networks that provide the best result.
LEM2: Meneghetti et al. [29] mention that there are numerous models that propose to solve the problems of diagnosis and management of diabetes, they propose H2RM, an LEM2-based hybrid model, is used to simultaneously control blood glucose for types 1 and 2, and yields an average and balanced precision of 95.9%.
Fuzzy hierarchy: For their part, [30] mention that by 2035 the prevalence of diabetes will grow by 55% compared to 2013, thus they propose computational intelligence using a fuzzy hierarchy model that allows an early detection of diabetes. They compared the information in the model with the doctor’s decision. Bearing a similarity of 87.46% with the statement of the doctors.
Distributed algorithms: Finally, Wang et al. [31] indicate that with the growth of Big Data and the large amount of health information, it is necessary to structure and emphasize the size of this information for a possible solution, so they use an analytical predictive algorithm to predict the prevalence of types of diabetes and complications associated with the type of treatment provided.

11.3 Proposed Model

11.3.1 Predictive Models in Health
Development of a predictive model for type 2 diabetes mellitus using clinical data and genetics [32] indicates that there are recent genetic studies that provide convincing evidence that the location of a gene and the polymorphism of a group of molecules are a primary cause of type 2 diabetes. The construction of a prediction model of type 2 diabetes using clinical and genetic data is predicated on this assumption [33].
For the development of the model, a statistical method of sampling of 684 participants was used, where 472 were random subjects and 212 were subjects with type 2 diabetes mellitus. The average age was 64.1 years for the randomized and 64.5 years for the diagnosed patients with diabetes, so the ages were not significantly different. The 684 participants were subjected to a sampling of 499 groups of molecules previously associated with type 2 diabetes mellitus. Factors that may intercede in the development of the disease were also considered, such as body mass index and triglyceride count, to refine the result by determining an incorrect classification ratio of type 2 diabetes mellitus of 27.98 ± 2.76 for all the tested algorithms. To improve the results of the classification, a multi-scenario adjustment model was used, selecting risk factors that allow smaller groupings according to common characteristics to perform a pattern recognition analysis. Based on this, three groups were determined: the first contained factors associated with obesity (body mass index, weight, and ratio between waist and hip), the second contained the highest ratios of glucose, insulin resistance, cholesterol and hemoglobin, and the third included the highest values of body mass index, weight, and heart rate of work. Figure 11.3 shows the dispersion between triglycerides, body mass index and incidence of type 2 diabetes mellitus.
The prevalence of type 2 diabetes mellitus was calculated using statistical tree decision models, which revealed that in men and women, the prevalence was about the same for groups 2 and 3, but it was smaller in group 1 [34]. In the graphic below, you can see a scatter plot that compares triglyceride levels, body mass index, and the incidence of type 2 diabetes:
In clusters 1-3, 2-3 and 3-3, we can identify patients suffering from the disease and we notice a trend toward high triglycerides and body mass index for these clusters.
Based on the statistical analysis and classification of factors, the following risk factors for developing the disease were determined:

Clinical information: height, body mass index, waist circumference, blood pressure, cholesterol, triglyceride count and glucose level.
Family and personal history: hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, cerebrovascular diseases and other vascular diseases
Group of molecules polymorphism (SNP): 499 SNPs were identified in 87 candidate genes associated with type 2 diabetes mellitus.

Figure 11.3 Dispersion between triglycerides, body mass index and incidence of type 2 diabetes mellitus.
To build an optimal predictive model, it was initially chosen to use the chi-square statistical method, however, very high error rates were obtained (between 28.24 and 52.69%) due to the unequal distribution of genotyping types (within the 499 SNPs there were 70-75% dominant genotypes, 5-15% recessive and the rest were heterozygous, in addition, the clinical data was variable while the data of groups of molecules was relatively static, so when comparing both factors an extremely important risk factor could be disregarded. The models built using decision trees and machine-learning methods did not show many differences due to the same factors. To obtain For a better result, it would be necessary to adequately quantify the SNPs to initially classify patients into high-risk and low-risk groups. The analysis concludes that the error rates were higher than expected, so it is recommended to use larger samples and better analysis strategies to integrate genotyping to objectively quantify high-risk SNPs [35].

11.4 Experimental Results and Discussion

11.4.1 Prediction of Diabetes with Artificial Neural Networks Supervised Learning Algorithms
We propose using multilayer neural networks to obtain successful predictions of diabetes compared to traditional methods, based on the fact that there are neural network algorithms that have been proven to be useful for diagnosing heart disease, tuberculosis, pneumonia and lung cancer. A sample of 250 diabetes patients was used, among which these values include things like blood pressure, urine acidity, and creatinine, which are considered numerical values. To find the input-output connection via training utilizing a collection of data, supervised learning is defined as a methodology, learning from the output based on the error signal that is emitted according to the diagnosis, continuing until the minimum value is identified error allowed.
Algorithms available in the MATLAB neural network tools were used, using the following:

Fletcher–Powell conjugate gradient method.
Polak–Ribiére conjugate gradient method.
Scaled conjugate gradient method.

To identify the relationship between desired results and obtained results, a regression analysis was used where a correlation coefficient R was obtained, with 1 being an ideal result. Next, the algorithms’ performances are displayed the regression test carried out on this case was the basis for the conclusion. We obtain the following sketch. In the figures below, we identify the correlation coefficient R corresponding to each algorithm respectively, reflected by the dotted line. We can identify that the conjugate gradient algorithm scaled almost above the reference line, indicating a result close between what was expected and what was predicted. Figure 11.4 shows the dispersion of algorithm results versus the correlation coefficient R and the Figure 11.5 shows the algorithm performance measurement.
The objective of the study is to determine the algorithm that has the best performance with the collected data. The results of the coefficient R are as follows shown in Table 11.1.
Identifying that the scaled conjugate gradient algorithm is by far the best performer in predicting diabetes compared to the poor performances of the Polak-Ribiére and Fletcher-Powell algorithms.

Figure 11.4 Dispersion of algorithm results versus the correlation coefficient R. Label (a) could represent a baseline or standard algorithm against which others are compared. It may show minimal variation in results across different R values, indicating consistent performance. Label (b) might depict an alternative algorithm that has a varying degree of dispersion, perhaps indicating that its performance is more sensitive to changes in the correlation coefficient. Label (c) could illustrate another variant or an improved algorithm that possibly offers a reduced dispersion of results, suggesting it is more robust or optimized for varying conditions of the correlation coefficient.

11.4.2 Improving the Prediction Ratio of Diabetes Diagnoses Using Fuzzy Logic and Neural Networks
It indicates that many approaches have been made in predicting diabetes but that there has never been a special focus on the precision of the models developed.
Existing systems are limited and provide accurate results if high-quality, standardized data sets are used, or few data sets are used. It is under this premise that the paper presents a new approach to the diagnosis of diabetes employing two phases to predict the state of diabetes: the first is to use fuzzy logic, neural networks and reasoning based on cases as an individual approach and the second is apply an algorithm to the values obtained in the first phase, resulting in a prediction with greater objectivity.
A framework is proposed in which a user enters a system and is forced to enter data on its characteristics that can demonstrate a willingness to develop diabetes, and then analyze these parameters. The three approaches mentioned in the initial phase are normalized and applied: fuzzy logic, neural networks and reasoning based on cases, saving these results and finally applying an algorithm to derive the results, generating a final diagnostic report.
The steps followed for data analysis are as follows:

Information gathering
Data such as age, gender, family illness information, high blood pressure medications, tobacco use, diet, physical activity, body mass index, urination frequency, increased hunger, thirst level, poor wound healing, lifestyle and itching.
Information analysis through neural network techniques:
Using the back-propagation algorithm: the information is fed to the input layer consisting of a number n of neurons, the activities of the hidden layer depend on the
input layer and consists of n + 1 neurons, and the output layer represents 0 or 1, being 1 when the patient is affected by diabetes.
Using concepts of fuzzy logic: the data set is defined as indicated above and it is given a value based on the comparison between a patient with diabetes and a healthy one, being in a range between 0 and 2. Then the rules are defined decision according to each parameter: for example, for ages under 35 we can determine a “low” fuzzy set due to the low propensity to develop the disease. In this way and using AND and OR connectors to evaluate the patient’s conditions and the result according to the weighting of each variable.
Applying reasoning concepts based on cases: two phases are defined; one is predata processing and one is query processing.
First, knowledge and data are acquired and in the second, it is processed based on a set of data already defined to identify the propensity of the patient to develop the disease.
Rule-based algorithm application:
Based on the application of the information analysis approach outlined above, a rule-based algorithm is used to discern between certain and doubtful information, ensuring the final prediction.

It is concluded that the approach proposed through the 3 information analysis fronts is more useful than traditional approaches due to the reduction of errors through the mixture of information analysis and the application of a rule-based algorithm. Likewise, the framework would be beneficial because it allows the patient to obtain information quickly (in case this is implemented in a system) and that it can act in a way that combats those variables that make it prone to the development of the disease.

11.4.3 ARIC: Type 2 Diabetes Risk Predictive Model
A recognition of type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM) as a growing epidemic is vital for successful implementation of initiatives to prevent the onset of the condition. Many research investigations have shown that multivariate prediction models are equally effective as those that measure post-2-hour glucose exposure at predicting who will acquire diabetes. On the other hand, no studies assessing the capacity of the functions to correctly predict the likelihood of acquiring type 2 diabetes have been conducted so far.
Based on this, the authors examine the effectiveness of three predictive models to determine if it is possible to estimate the risk of type 2 diabetes in the Asian population and whether it is feasible to use this model in Asian culture. The San Antonio Health investigation (SAHS), Atherosclerosis Risk in Communities Study (ARIC), and Framingham Offspring Study (FRAM) models were employed in this investigation. To verify the models in the Asian context, data from a 1992 study (Singapore Prospective Study Programme, in National Health Survey-NHS. 1992) that included Singaporean Chinese, Malaysian, and Asian Indian persons was used. The authors investigated three published prediction models (Comp 1, Comp 2, and the local NHS 1992 model) in order to find out which one performed better. To date, published multivariate prediction functions have been shown to differ from the estimates created locally.
In general, the published ARIC model marginalizes only slightly better than the published SAHS model. Published models show poor calibration compared to local models that have good calibration properties. The Framingham model shows the worst result when it comes to recalibration, ARIC and SAHS have the best results, but SAHS shows an overestimation of cases with type 2 diabetes compared to the ARIC model. The analysis carried out by the authors suggests that the best model of the 3 evaluated is the ARIC Model.

11.4.4 Evaluation of Neural Network Algorithms for Prediction Models of Type 2 Diabetes
Mention how diabetes is a disease that is growing the fastest and is a global issue. The intricate interdependencies of several variables make it challenging to diagnose type 2 diabetes in its early stages. For this reason, they suggest that it is essential to create a diagnostic support system that aids medical professionals in their diagnostic procedure. According to the authors, an analysis may provide a less precise conclusion if the training data set includes unnecessary categorization features. Because of this, data pre-processing is necessary to increase the accuracy of the analysis; otherwise, neural networks may operate at a very sluggish speed. The UCI machine learning library’s Pima Indian dataset was used to extract attributes from the diabetes dataset. Nine factors make up this data collection, which distinguishes the accuracy of different algorithms.
The authors made a comparison of the precision rate of N algorithms, of which some algorithms such as KNN, PCA, LDA, GDA allow data to be pre-processed, this allows the missing information to be completed according to patterns detected by each algorithm. Each carrying out their task differently from the other. They show how the LDA Morlet Wavelet Support Vector Machine Classifier or LDA-MWSVM algorithm, which has Linear Discriminant Analysis or LDA as its pre-processing algorithm, significantly improves its precision ratio.
They conclude that the pre-processing of the data and the extraction characteristic together with Neural Network models improves the accuracy classification.

11.4.5 Reliable and Objective Recommendation System for the Diagnosis of Chronic Diseases
It proposes an accurate and reliable chronic disease diagnosis system to provide 24/7 remote monitoring. In the formulation of its hypothesis, it is indicated that if the risk of chronic diseases of a patient is predicted with high precision, an improvement in the patient’s health conditions and adjustments in their lifestyle should be expected, reducing coverage costs of health.
To increase precision, it is necessary to reduce false positives and false negatives and that is why the origin and complexity of the collected medical data is considered (understanding by complexity to nonstandardized data, with a high number of parameters or missing). It is indicated that medical information is generated by various sources and by various people, making it prone to different types of errors such as missing or erroneous data, this being an extremely important challenge for the effectiveness of the diagnosis. In the paper, a decision tree classification scope is proposed to increase the effectiveness of the diagnosis, with proven results on medical data.

Training data: it is information from previous diabetic patients and consists of 935 records from Oman hospitals, within which there are attributes, values and diagnoses of the doctor, using a scale of 1 to 10 where 1-2 represent excellent prognosis and 9–10 represent critical condition.
Demographic data: data on age, name, level of education, type of device connecting to the system and type of connectivity.
Patient medical data: blood sugar level, blood pressure and weight.

Within the identified decision tree classification, the Random Forest (RF) classification method is used for the analysis and segmentation of the information entered: a distinction is made between related information based on the diagnostic scales, using these relationships to generate a predictive model to determine the patient’s diagnostic class. Based on the analysis and classification of these data, a numerical value is provided that represents the patient’s risk diagnosis of a disease. In addition, the diagnosis is taken and it is re-analyzed to now compare it against the diagnoses of other patients, identifying additional coincidences. According to the proposed flow, once this analysis is concluded, information is sent to the health providers who would approve the accuracy of the diagnosis and update the patient’s profile, sending mitigation actions against the factors that pose a risk of developing the disease and sending the information of the Diagnostics to the system to improve the way it learns and provides future diagnostics.
The proposed predictive model is compared to other decision tree models such as J48, Decision Stump and REP Tree. The information analyzed is from patients in Oman hospitals and consists of 935 patients and 13 attributes (including the doctor’s diagnostic scale).

For the evaluation of the RF model compared to the others, the MAE metric (measurement of absolute error) was used, dictated by the following formula:

where MAE represents the average of absolute errors ei, where ei = fi − yi, where fi is the diagnosis and yi is the actual diagnosis.

The mean error of 10 RF trees is 0.2877
RF has correctly demonstrated 99.7% of correctly classified cases

RF improves accuracy by managing the data internally through the aforementioned phases of analyzing its predictions once they have been issued: this approach to learning enriches the model and stimulates its accuracy. It is concluded that the RF method will generate effective prediction of risk of chronic diseases of patients who suffer from them, because it learns from its own results and enriches its future predictions. 11.5 Conclusion
Diabetes is a disease with the greatest growth, being a problem around the world. Diagnosing type 2 diabetes in the early stages is a difficult challenge to meet due to the complex interdependencies of several factors. It is for this reason that they indicate that it is necessary to develop a diagnostic support system, which is beneficial for medical practitioners in their diagnostic process. if the data set under training contains irrelevant classification attributes, the analysis may produce a less accurate result. That is why data pre-processing is required to improve the precision of the analysis, without pre-processing neural networks could be very slow. To increase precision, it is necessary to reduce false positives and false negatives and that is why the origin and complexity of the collected medical data is considered (understanding by complexity to non-standardized data, with a high number of parameters or missing). It is indicated that medical information is generated by various sources and by various people, making it prone to different types of errors such as missing or erroneous data, this being an extremely important challenge for the effectiveness of the diagnosis. In the paper, a decision tree classification scope is proposed to increase the effectiveness of the diagnosis, with proven results on medical data.

Wang, L., Pedersen, P.C., Strong, D.M., Tulu, B., Agu, E., Ignotz, R., Smartphone-Based Wound Assessment System for Patients With Diabetes. IEEE Trans. Biomed. Eng., 62, 2, 477–488, Feb. 2015, doi: 10.1109/ TBME.2014.2358632.
Ngo, C.Q., Chai, R., Nguyen, T.V., Jones, T.W., Nguyen, H.T., Electroencephalogram Spectral Moments for the Detection of Nocturnal Hypoglycemia. IEEE J. Biomed. Health Inf., 24, 5, 1237–1245, May 2020, doi: 10.1109/JBHI.2019.2931782.
Usman, I. and Almejalli, K.A., Intelligent Automated Detection of Microaneurysms in Fundus Images Using Feature-Set Tuning. IEEE Access, 8, 65187–65196, 2020, doi: 10.1109/ACCESS.2020.2985543.
Sarki, R., Ahmed, K., Wang, H., Zhang, Y., Automatic Detection of Diabetic Eye Disease Through Deep Learning Using Fundus Images: A Survey. IEEE Access, 8, 151133–151149, 2020, doi: 10.1109/ACCESS.2020.3015258.
Meneghetti, L., Facchinetti, A., Favero, S.D., Model-Based Detection and Classification of Insulin Pump Faults and Missed Meal Announcements in Artificial Pancreas Systems for Type 1 Diabetes Therapy. IEEE Trans. Biomed. Eng., 68, 1, 170–180, Jan. 2021, doi: 10.1109/TBME.2020.3004270.

12Diagnosis of Neurological Disease Using Bioinspired Algorithms
Inam Ul Haq
Faculty of Engineering and Technology, SGT University Gurugram, Haryana, India

Abstract
Neurological diseases have a significant impact on individuals, families, and society. Accurate and timely diagnosis is crucial for effective treatment and management. However, traditional diagnostic methods are often expensive and time- consuming. Recent advancements in bioinspired algorithms offer new possibilities for the diagnosis of neurological diseases. This chapter provides an overview of the role of bioinspired algorithms in neurological disease diagnosis. The chapter begins with an introduction to bioinspired algorithms, covering various types commonly used in healthcare, such as genetic algorithms, neural networks, ant colony optimization, and particle swarm optimization. The applications of bio-inspired algorithms in healthcare are explored, emphasizing their contributions to medical image analysis, disease diagnosis, treatment optimization, and personalized medicine. The chapter then focuses on neurological disease diagnosis, providing an overview of common diseases and current diagnostic techniques. It reviews traditional diagnostic methods and their limitations, highlighting the need for alternative approaches. The application of bioinspired algorithms in neurological disease diagnosis is discussed in detail. Genetic algorithms optimize feature selection and classification algorithms, demonstrated through case studies and research findings. Neural networks are explored for their potential in improving disease diagnosis accuracy through bioinspired optimization techniques. Other bioinspired algorithms, such as ant colony optimization and particle swarm optimization, are also examined for their applications in feature selection, clustering analysis, and diagnostic model optimization. Finally, the chapter addresses the challenges and limitations associated with the use of bioinspired algorithms in neurological disease diagnosis. It emphasizes the need for further research and development in this emerging field. Overall, bioinspired algorithms hold great promise in advancing the field of neurological disease diagnosis, offering new opportunities for accurate and efficient diagnosis.
Keywords: Neurological disease, bioinspired algorithms, artificial intelligence, genetic algorithms

12.1 Introduction

12.1.1 Neurological Diseases
Neurological diseases encompass a diverse range of disorders that affect the brain, spinal cord, and peripheral nervous system. These conditions have a profound impact on individuals, families, and society as a whole, posing significant challenges in terms of diagnosis, treatment, and management. Neurological diseases can lead to a wide array of symptoms, including cognitive impairment, movement disorders, sensory deficits, and even life-threatening complications. Understanding the complexities of neurological diseases requires exploring the intricate workings of the nervous system [1]. A sophisticated network of specialized cells known as neurons makes up the nervous system., which transmit and process electrical and chemical signals. It is in charge of directing and coordinating body processes, as well as enabling higher cognitive processes. Neurological diseases can arise from various causes, including genetic factors, infections, immune system disorders, trauma, toxic exposure, and age-related degeneration. Some neurological diseases, such as Parkinson’s disease and Alzheimer’s disease are both referred to as neurodegenerative illnesses by progressive loss of neurons and subsequent decline in neurological function. Others, like epilepsy and multiple sclerosis, involve abnormalities in the function or structure of the nervous system [2]. One of the major challenges in dealing with neurological diseases is their diverse nature and the wide spectrum of symptoms they present. Each neurological disorder has unique characteristics, requiring specialized diagnostic approaches and treatment strategies.
Figure 12.1 shows the common neurological diseases, and there are many more conditions within the field of neurology.
Moreover, neurological diseases often exhibit significant heterogeneity, with variations in symptoms, disease progression, and treatment responses among affected individuals. This heterogeneity underscores the need for personalized and targeted approaches to diagnosis and treatment. Accurate and timely diagnosis of neurological diseases is of utmost importance for several reasons. First and foremost, an accurate diagnosis allows healthcare professionals to provide appropriate and targeted treatment interventions. Many neurological diseases are chronic or progressive in nature, and early intervention can help slow disease progression, manage alleviate symptoms, and raise general quality of life [3]. In some cases, such as stroke or traumatic brain injury, prompt diagnosis is crucial for immediate lifesaving interventions. Furthermore, accurate diagnosis enables researchers and scientists to study the underlying mechanisms of neurological diseases, paving the way for the development of new therapeutic strategies and interventions. It also facilitates the identification of biomarkers, which are measurable indicators that can aid in the diagnosis, prognosis, and monitoring of disease progression. However, diagnosing neurological diseases can be challenging due to the complex and overlapping nature of their symptoms. Many neurological disorders lack specific diagnostic tests, making diagnosis primarily reliant on clinical evaluation, patient history, and the expertise of healthcare professionals [4]. Consequently, misdiagnosis or delayed diagnosis is not uncommon, leading to significant implications for patients and their families. Advancements in medical technology, imaging techniques, and laboratory investigations have significantly improved the diagnostic capabilities for many neurological diseases. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), computed tomography (CT) scans, electroencephalography (EEG), and cerebrospinal fluid analysis are some of the tools commonly used in the diagnostic process. However, these tests are often expensive, time-consuming, and may not be readily accessible in all healthcare settings.

Figure 12.1 Common neurological diseases.
The creation and use of bioinspired algorithms for neurological illness detection has gained popularity in recent years. Natural occurrences and processes, including genetic evolution, neural networks, swarm intelligence, and optimization methods, serve as the basis for bioinspired algorithms. In order to handle complicated issues, these algorithms imitate the behavior of natural systems, and they have demonstrated promising outcomes in a variety of fields, including healthcare [5].
Bioinspired algorithms have the potential to enhance the accuracy, efficiency, and accessibility of neurological disease diagnosis. They can aid in the identification of relevant biomarkers, optimize diagnostic models, and improve the analysis of medical images and patient data. By harnessing the power of computational techniques and artificial intelligence, bioinspired algorithms offer new possibilities for early detection, accurate diagnosis, and personalized treatment strategies for neurological diseases [6].

12.1.2 Introduction to Bioinspired Algorithms
Bioinspired algorithms, also known as nature-inspired algorithms or computational intelligence techniques, are computational methods inspired by biological systems and processes found in nature. These algorithms imitate the behavior, principles, and mechanisms observed in natural organisms or ecosystems to solve complex optimization and problem-solving tasks. By drawing inspiration from nature, bioinspired algorithms offer innovative solutions to a wide range of problems in various domains, including healthcare.
Principles and Characteristics of Bioinspired Algorithms:

Inspiration from nature: bioinspired algorithms derive their principles and methodologies from natural phenomena, such as the behavior of animals, the structure of ecosystems, or the processes within biological systems.
Optimization and problem solving: bioinspired algorithms excel in solving optimization problems, where the goal is to find the best solution from a large search space. They can also address complex problem-solving tasks by mimicking the adaptive and evolutionary mechanisms found in nature.
Parallelism and distributed processing: many bioinspired algorithms exhibit parallel and distributed processing, emulating the cooperative behavior seen in biological systems. This allows for efficient exploration of solution spaces and better scalability for handling large-scale problems.
Robustness and adaptation: bioinspired algorithms often possess robustness and adaptability, allowing them to handle noisy or uncertain environments. They can adapt to changing conditions, optimize performance, and recover from failures, resembling the resilience seen in natural systems.
Exploration and exploitation: bioinspired algorithms strike a balance between exploration (searching for new solutions) and exploitation (using existing knowledge) to efficiently navigate the solution space and avoid getting trapped in local optima.

12.1.3 Types of Bioinspired Algorithms Commonly Used in Healthcare
Bioinspired algorithms have found applications in healthcare for various purposes. Here, in Figure 12.2, there are some commonly used types of bioinspired algorithms in healthcare:

Figure 12.2 Some commonly used types of bioinspired algorithms in healthcare.

Genetic Algorithms (GA): GAs are optimization algorithms that draw inspiration from the laws of natural selection. In the context of healthcare, GA has proven to be valuable in several applications. One prominent use is in optimizing treatment plans for individual patients. By considering various treatment options, GA can search for the most effective combination and dosage of medications or therapies based on the patient’s unique characteristics and medical history [7]. This approach helps healthcare professionals personalize treatment and improve patient outcomes. Additionally, GA plays a significant role in drug discovery. The process of identifying new drugs can be time-consuming and costly. GA offers a computational approach to explore large chemical spaces and optimize molecular structures with desirable properties. By simulating evolution and natural selection, GA can generate novel drug candidates or modify existing compounds to enhance their efficacy or reduce side effects. This aids in accelerating the drug discovery process and potentially leads to the development of more effective treatments [8].
Artificial neural networks (ANN): Computer models known as artificial neural networks (ANNs) are influenced by the design and operation of biological neural networks. In healthcare, ANNs have gained significant attention and are used for various purposes. One key application of ANNs is in disease diagnosis and prognosis [5]. By training on large datasets of patient information, ANNs can learn patterns and relationships within the data and make accurate predictions about the presence or progression of diseases. They can analyze diverse inputs, such as patient demographics, symptoms, and medical test results, to provide diagnostic insights and assist healthcare professionals in decision making. Medical imaging analysis is another area where ANNs excel. They can be trained to interpret and analyze medical images, such as X-rays, MRIs, and CT scans. ANNs can automatically detect and classify abnormalities or assist in image segmentation, where specific structures or regions of interest are identified and delineated. This capability enhances the accuracy and efficiency of medical image interpretation, aiding in early detection and treatment planning. ANNs are also utilized for drug discovery and development [2]. They can be employed to predict the activity and toxicity of potential drug compounds, aiding in the selection and optimization of drug candidates. By learning from vast amounts of chemical and biological data, ANNs can identify novel drug–target interactions, analyze molecular structures, and provide insights into drug design and optimization.
Particle swarm optimization (PSO): PSO is a population- based optimization algorithm that is inspired by the social behavior of bird flocking or fish schooling. In the context of healthcare, PSO has been applied to various problems and has shown promising results. One common application of PSO in healthcare is medical image segmentation. PSO can be used to optimize the parameters of image segmentation algorithms, such as determining optimal thresholds or region-growing parameters [9]. By iteratively updating the position and velocity of particles representing potential solutions, PSO searches the parameter space to find the optimal segmentation result, leading to improved accuracy and efficiency in medical image analysis. PSO is also used for feature selection in healthcare data analysis. In many healthcare datasets, there are numerous features or variables that may not all be relevant for a specific task. PSO can be employed to identify the subset of features that contribute the most to a particular prediction or classification task. By evaluating the fitness of different feature combinations, PSO guides the search process and selects the most informative features, reducing dimensionality and improving the performance of healthcare models. Furthermore, PSO has been utilized in optimizing treatment parameters. For instance, in radiation therapy planning, PSO can optimize the beam angles and intensities to maximize the radiation dose to the tumor while minimizing the dose to surrounding healthy tissues. By iteratively adjusting the particle positions representing different treatment plans, PSO helps find an optimal solution that balances treatment effectiveness and patient safety [10].
Ant colony optimization (ACO): ACO is a metaheuristic algorithm inspired by the foraging behavior of ants. It is commonly used in various healthcare applications, offering effective solutions to optimization problems. One primary application of ACO in healthcare is healthcare routing and scheduling. ACO can optimize the routing of healthcare resources, such as ambulances, healthcare personnel, or medical supplies to minimize travel distance or time while ensuring efficient delivery of healthcare services. Additionally, ACO can help in scheduling healthcare tasks, such as patient appointments or operating room schedules, by considering factors like priority, resource availability, and minimizing waiting times. Another area where ACO is applied is in healthcare network optimization. ACO can optimize the layout and organization of healthcare facilities, such as hospitals or clinics, to improve accessibility and efficiency. By considering factors like patient demand, transportation connectivity, and resource allocation, ACO can help in designing healthcare networks that provide optimal coverage and meet the healthcare needs of a population [9]. ACO is also used in healthcare data clustering and classification. By mimicking the behavior of ants laying pheromone trails to guide their colony, ACO can identify natural groupings or clusters within healthcare datasets. This aids in patient stratification or identifying patterns in disease data. Furthermore, ACO can be employed for feature selection in healthcare data analysis, assisting in identifying the most informative and relevant features for prediction or classification tasks.
Artificial Immune Systems (AIS): AIS are computational models inspired by the human immune system’s mechanisms and processes. In healthcare, AIS has been applied to various areas and has shown promise in solving complex problems. One significant application of AIS is in anomaly detection and intrusion detection systems. By mimicking the immune system’s ability to recognize foreign or abnormal entities, AIS algorithms can detect and identify unusual patterns or behaviors in healthcare data. This can help in identifying potential health risks, fraudulent activities, or anomalies in medical records, improving the overall security and integrity of healthcare systems [10]. AIS is also used in disease classification and diagnosis. By leveraging the principles of self/non–self-discrimination, AIS algorithms can analyze patient data and identify patterns indicative of specific diseases or conditions. This can aid in accurate and early diagnosis, allowing for timely interventions and improved patient outcomes. Furthermore, AIS has been applied to optimize vaccination strategies. By simulating the immune response to pathogens, AIS algorithms can determine optimal vaccination schedules, dosage levels, and priority groups. This helps in optimizing immunization programs, preventing the spread of infectious diseases, and maximizing the effectiveness of vaccination campaigns. Another area where AIS is employed is in optimizing healthcare processes and resource allocation. By considering the immune system’s ability to allocate resources based on the urgency and importance of different tasks, AIS algorithms can assist in healthcare resource management [11]. This includes optimizing bed allocation, staff scheduling, and resource utilization, ultimately leading to more efficient and cost-effective healthcare delivery.
Evolutionary algorithms (EAs): EAs are computational algorithms inspired by the principles of biological evolution and natural selection. In healthcare, EA has been widely used for solving optimization problems and has shown effectiveness in various applications. One prominent application of EA in healthcare is optimizing treatment protocols. EA can be employed to optimize the dosage and scheduling of medications or therapies for individual patients. By simulating the evolutionary process of selection and adaptation, EA explores a population of potential treatment plans and evolves them over generations to find the most effective and personalized treatment strategies [4].
Swarm intelligence algorithms: SIAs are computational algorithms inspired by the collective behavior of social insect colonies or other swarming organisms. In healthcare, SIAs have been applied to solve complex problems and optimize various processes. One common application of SIAs in healthcare is medical data clustering. SIAs, such as particle swarm optimization (PSO) and ant colony optimization (ACO), can be used to identify natural groupings or clusters within healthcare datasets [12]. By simulating the collective behavior of swarming organisms, SIAs help in discovering patterns and relationships in large and complex datasets, facilitating data analysis and decision making. SIAs are also utilized in healthcare resource allocation and scheduling. By mimicking the collaborative behavior of swarming organisms, SIAs can optimize the allocation and utilization of healthcare resources such as hospital beds, healthcare personnel, or medical equipment. This helps in maximizing resource efficiency, minimizing waiting times, and improving the overall healthcare service delivery. SIAs have been applied to optimize healthcare routing and logistics. By simulating the movement and interaction patterns of swarming organisms, SIAs can optimize the routing of ambulances, healthcare supplies, or medical samples. This ensures efficient and timely delivery of healthcare services and improves response times in emergency situations. SIAs are also used in healthcare optimization problems, such as healthcare facility location planning and healthcare network design. By leveraging the principles of swarm intelligence, SIAs can identify optimal locations for healthcare facilities, considering factors like population distribution, accessibility, and demand. Additionally, SIAs assist in designing efficient healthcare networks that ensure effective coverage and accessibility to healthcare services [13].

These algorithms leverage principles inspired by biological systems to solve complex healthcare problems. Each algorithm has its own strengths and applications within the healthcare domain.

12.1.4 Advantages and Limitations of Bioinspired Algorithms
Bioinspired algorithms have gained significant attention and have been successfully applied to various fields due to their unique advantages. However, like any computational technique, they also have certain limitations. In this discussion, we will explore the advantages and limitations of bioinspired algorithms in detail.
Advantages:

Optimization and problem solving: bioinspired algorithms excel in solving complex optimization problems. They can efficiently explore large solution spaces and find near- optimal or optimal solutions. These algorithms leverage the power of natural selection, adaptation, and swarm intelligence to handle complex, multidimensional, and nonlinear problems.
Robustness and adaptability: bioinspired algorithms often exhibit robustness and adaptability. They can handle noisy or uncertain environments, making them suitable for real-world applications where data may be incomplete or contain errors. These algorithms can adapt and optimize their performance in dynamic environments, allowing them to respond effectively to changing conditions.
Parallel and distributed processing: many bioinspired algorithms inherently possess parallel and distributed processing capabilities. They can explore multiple solutions simultaneously and leverage collective intelligence. This enables efficient exploration of large search spaces, faster convergence to solutions, and scalability for handling large-scale problems.
Exploration and exploitation: bioinspired algorithms strike a balance between exploration and exploitation.
